Two /apps editor traps (PR #5243 review, code-read on main — the PR itself merged as 6d394b7a): `resolve_arg()` resolves templates with `value.format(**self.args)` and catches **only `KeyError`**, so an unbalanced brace (`abc{def`) raises `ValueError` and a positional `{0}` raises `IndexError` — `WebInterface.resolve_value_raw()` calls it for every string containing `{` on the `/apps` page render path, so a non-credential value like that is *suspected, not reproduced* to 500 the whole page (credentials escape on the page only because `mask_secret_args()` has already turned them into `xxx`); and the editor addresses nested values by dotted path (`parse_yaml_path()` splits on `.` and `[n]`) while `render_type()` builds `data-nested-path` from the literal key, so a dict key that literally contains `.` or `[` cannot be edited correctly — the save targets the wrong node or raises. The quote-bearing-value truncation (`value="${currentValue}"` in the JS) that #5243 fixed — input values now set via `input.value` and `escapeHtml` — matters only on pre-#5243 trees. **Plugins: endpoints work, nav is hardcoded (GH#5367, code-verified on main).** Plugin `on_web_start` hooks fire in `WebInterface.start()` (`web.py`) *before* the `registered_endpoints` loop mounts routes — the plugin system is initialised early (`predbat.py`) exactly for that — so a plugin page that 404s is an `on_web_start` problem, not a routing one. But `get_header_html()` (`web_helper.py`) hardcodes the nav menu with no plugin extension point, and `registered_endpoints` is consumed only by `start()` — no page lists them — so a plugin page is reachable only by typing its URL, despite `docs/plugins.md` promising "additional web interfaces and dashboards". "My plugin's page loads but I can't get to it from the Predbat UI" → the gap is nav/UI exposure, not the endpoint mechanism; fix shape is an opt-in plugin nav API (label+path, not auto-listing `registered_endpoints` — plugins register API-only paths too), matching the existing conditional-nav precedents (the Chat link's gate, "Metrics"). **Three header-CSS / menu traps (PR #5585, 2026-10-17, each hit while building the Settings/Advanced sub-menus):** (a) the shared header CSS in `get_header_html()` sets `p { white-space: nowrap }` for every page, so a new page's paragraphs run off-screen on a phone unless the page overrides it for its own scope; (b) dark mode paints **every** `<button>` red via `body.dark-mode button { background-color: #cc3333 !important }` (meant for the restart button), and the `!important` beats any more specific rule without one - a button that should not be red needs its own `!important` dark-mode colours; (c) WebKit (Safari, the iOS companion app) clips a `position: fixed` element to a fixed ancestor with `overflow` set, and `.menu-bar` is exactly that (`position: fixed; overflow-x: auto`), so a popup inside the bar opens invisibly on iPhone while desktop Chrome shows it - which is why the sub-menu popups are siblings of `.menu-bar`, placed under their `data-menu` toggle. "The menu does nothing on my phone" → check the popup has not been moved back inside the bar; headless Chrome cannot reproduce it, only real WebKit can. **`get_status`/MCP tools report an apps.yaml arg as null or 0 — wrong resolver (GH#5732, fixed in PR #5742, merged 2026-10-26; keep the pre-fix reading for older logs):** `get_ha_config()` (`userinterface.py`) resolves names against the `CONFIG_ITEMS` HA-entity index only, returns `(None, default)` on a miss **with no log at the call site or in the resolver**, and `num_cars` is an apps.yaml arg (`fetch.py`), not a config item — `get_status` reported it null while `get_plan` read `self.num_cars` directly; #5742 reads `self.base.num_cars` (the same source `get_plan` uses). Class generalisation: any "AI/MCP tool reports X as null/0" report → first check whether X is a `CONFIG_ITEMS` entity or an apps.yaml arg; settle it by searching for a `"name": "<arg>"` entry in `config.py`, not bare name hits (`grep num_cars config.py` returns ~10 hits that are all per-car `enable:`-condition expressions). Only `debug_enable`, `set_read_only` and `mode` among `get_status`'s fields are genuine config entities. Nothing pins these tool fields — the suite stubs `get_ha_config` to return defaults — so a green suite proves nothing there. | `web_*`, `web_mcp`, `web_chat` |
| Plugins (`plugin_system.py`) | "Plugin logs as loaded successfully but does nothing (hooks never fire, its entity goes stale)" → read the `Initialising plugin class (name-based): <X>` line and check *which* class `X` is: `load_plugin()` takes the **first name-sorted** `inspect.getmembers()` match whose name ends `"Plugin"`, so the imported base `PredBatPlugin` — every plugin does `from plugin_system import PredBatPlugin` — can win the match whenever it sorts before the plugin's own class (e.g. anything starting Q-Z); it is a plain class with no ABC, construction succeeds silently and the base's `register_hooks()` is a no-op (verified against main 2026-10-29). Fall-through: a truthy `PREDBAT_PLUGIN` attribute, then module-level `initialize_plugin()`; discovery scans only files literally ending `_plugin.py`, single-level, in three default dirs. Test trap from the review (PR #4319, open at writing): a probe class must sort *after* the base or the bug hides — a class sorting before it passes with **and** without the fix; to reach strategy 2 the file must end `_plugin.py` and the class must not end `Plugin`. That PR adds `__module__` guards to strategies 1/2, with two review-flagged consequences worth remembering if it re-surfaces: the guard silently breaks the re-export pattern (class defined in a helper module and imported into the `_plugin.py` file) even when it previously worked — `docs/plugins.md` never states the class must be defined in that file — and strategy 2 re-runs `inspect.getmembers()` and re-instantiates, so a plugin raising in `__init__` after side effects is constructed twice. |
| Self-update (`github.py`, `download.py`) | The whole update path calls the GitHub REST API **unauthenticated** - the release check (`github.py:126`) and the file listing (`download.py:79`) - so it shares the 60-requests-per-hour-per-IP unauthenticated quota with everything else behind that egress IP. Behind CGNAT, a VPN or a shared proxy that 403s even though Predbat's own volume is one check per two hours; failures are deliberately never cached, so it then retries every 5-minute cycle. The listing request's error reaches only the addon stdout via `print()`, not the HA log a user would paste, which is why the report reads as "it just won't update" (GH#4886). A phone-hotspot A/B is the cheap differential test. | none |
| Cloud / divergence modelling (`fetch.py`, `plan.py`) | Until v8.55.0 `step_data_history()`'s modulation was inert for every caller but one, through operator precedence: `int(...) + 1 if flip else 0` parses as `(int(...) + 1) if flip else 0`, a constant `0` whenever `flip` is False - and `plan.py`'s PV10 call was the sole `flip=True` caller. So `metric_cloud_enable` only ever perturbed the PV10 scenario, and `metric_load_divergence` - genuinely computed from load std-dev/mean in `output.py` - did nothing at all, on defaults-on settings, for as long as the block existed (GH#4870, evaluated directly rather than inferred; fixed in `f6c925d8` and then reworked again by the envelope model in `79f28f8f`). Worth knowing when reading a pre-v8.55.0 log or replaying an older debug dump: neither knob can be the explanation there. The review of the fix also found that switching the block *on* is the risky half - an empty `pv_forecast_minute10` pins `metric_cloud_coverage` at exactly 0.5 regardless of the weather, which is every non-Solcast user. |
| Load forecast & scenario hedging (`fetch.py`, `plan.py`) | Two forecast facts from the "battery empties before evening ends" family (GH#5198). With `days_previous_auto` (the default) the forward load is `compute_load_forecast_history()` — a per-5-minute-slot weighted **mean** using built-in weights (weekday-match 1.0/0.7/0.5 × recency 0.9→0.1 over the window, verified against the docstring on main); the user-tuned `days_previous_weight` list only drives the legacy per-day path that shapes the in-day comparison chart and the 8-hour load-divergence metric, not the forecast — start there on "my weights don't change anything" or "the forecast targets the mean" reports. The downside hedge is the PV10 scenario: its load series is stepped with `load_scaling10` (clamped in `fetch_config_options()` so `load_scaling90 <= load_scaling <= load_scaling10`), and `compute_metric()` blends the scenarios as a **signed weighted average** — `pv_metric10_weight` (default 0.15) plus the expert `pv_metric90_weight` (default 0.15, only when PV90 was simulated) — so a reporter who raised `load_scaling` alone has zero extra load in the PV10 scenario and the 0.15 weight hedges PV risk only; `load_scaling10` is the concrete lever. Load ML publishes a point forecast, no percentile band, and the scenario machinery is source-independent — switching to ML adds rate-awareness, not variance budgeting. Enhancement flagged on GH#5198, not built: size the pessimistic load from the 8-hour σ the divergence metric already computes. **New-install first-days gap fixed in PR #5553 (merged 2026-10-15, v9.4.0; GH#5548) — keep the mechanism for pre-#5553 logs:** under `days_previous_auto` `compute_load_forecast_history()` sampled only whole calendar days before today, so a fresh install's not-yet-recorded times of day had no sample at all (base load, previously 0) and the forecast was skipped entirely under a day of history; a slot with no previous-day sample now takes today's sample at that time-of-day once reached, then the recorded average of everything so far, then base load. Today never contributes where a previous day has data, so an established install is unaffected, and elapsed today-slots predating the install stay at base load (they used to read the recorded average and could push the in-day adjustment to its floor). | none |
| iBoost (`plan.py`) | `plan_iboost_smart()`'s inner rate-averaging loop indexed the *outer* loop's `minute` instead of the inner `slot_start`, so the "average" always collapsed to the rate at the window's start minute (GH#4817, fixed in PR #4821). Two things worth carrying forward: the `iboost_smart2`/`iboost_smart3` golden scenarios in `test_model.py` were pinned to the buggy output and had their expected values regenerated by the fix, and the `iboost_smart` unit test passed throughout because its fixture uses a flat rate that cannot expose an averaging bug. A sibling defect in the same function - a duplicate-slot guard that checks a stale loop variable and so never fires - was found and deliberately left unfixed. **GH#5454 (enhancement, verified by reading main):** the docs contradict the code on combining the modes - `docs/customisation.md` says iboost_smart "only applies when iboost_solar and iboost battery are both Off" while `fetch.py`'s gate is `((not iboost_solar) and (not iboost_charging)) or iboost_smart`, so the smart plan is built whatever iboost_solar says and `prediction.py` consumes `iboost_plan` without checking `iboost_solar` (the solar branch is additive on top) - quoting the docs as "the modes are exclusive" is the wrong answer to a "why did rates+iBoost both run" report. **The combination is grid-first:** `plan_iboost_smart()` fills each day's budget to `iboost_max_energy` with cheap-rate slots and has no PV input at all, the budget is consumed in time order in the prediction, so an overnight cheap slot spends the budget before midday solar and solar diverts only what no cheap-enough slot claimed - with the default `iboost_rate_threshold` the planner books the entire daily cap and solar gets nothing. `iboost_rate_threshold` min is -10 (`config.py`), so a ≤0p-only boost is already configurable in rates-only mode. Adjacent work: open PR #4756 (demand-forecast planning on `plan_iboost_smart()`) is where planner solar-awareness would build; mirror-image car ticket #5384; the `test_iboost.py` smart tests use a flat rate, so a solar-vs-rate priority bug cannot show. | `iboost_smart` |
| Component auto-config gate (`components.py`) | `components.py:705-708` decides whether to print a `Warn: Skipping <X> interface, missing required configuration` by checking whether *any* of the component's declared args appear anywhere in `args_from_apps_yaml` — not whether that component's own identifying args are set. `gecloud_data` lists `days_previous` among its constructor args, a core scheduling setting that ships uncommented in the default `config/apps.yaml`, so the gate is truthy and the warning fires on installs with no GE Cloud configuration at all (GH#4786). **That half is fixed** - `950dda36` / PR #4813, released in v8.54.0, made the gate skip args flagged `shared_config` (`components.py:781`) and flagged `days_previous` in the `gecloud_data` definition; re-driven through `Components.initialize(only="gecloud_data")` on 2026-08-31 with the reporter's exact config, no warning. The `solar` sibling **was** then reproduced and still fires: `pv_forecast_today/tomorrow/d3/d4` ship uncommented as `re:` regexes in `apps/predbat/config/apps.yaml:291-294`, so on a battery-only install their raw strings sit in `args_from_apps_yaml` while matching nothing, and the component warns it "needs at least one of" four args the user never wrote. `shared_config` alone cannot fix that one, because `pv_forecast_today` is itself in the component's `required_or`. A "component seems half-configured" report is worth checking against this gate before trusting the warning at face value. | none |
| Component health (`components.py`, `component_base.py`) | Three different signals, only one of which means "answering now". `count_errors` (`get_error_count()`) is a **lifetime counter** - incremented only when `run()` returns False or raises, never reset on success, and startup backoff books its expected retries through it - so "in error, N errors so far" can describe a long-recovered component. `api_started` means "run() returned truthy once", not "serving data": the Gateway component's `run()` deliberately returns True while `_auto_configured` is False so an offline device cannot stall startup (`gateway.py`), so a broker-less gateway flips `api_started=True` with zero data delivered. The current-health accessors are `is_alive()`/`last_updated_time()` (`components.py`), with a 60-minute staleness test. Separately, `record_status()` writes the raw message as the literal HA state of `sensor.predbat.status` (`dashboard_item()`) with nothing truncating on the path, and HA rejects states over 255 chars - long user-facing text belongs in an attribute (no limit) with a truncated state. Test trap: `update_status()` can call `record_status()` twice per cycle (charge window, then export window) and every call overwrites `self.current_status`, so a test that runs a full `update_status()` and asserts on `current_status` only ever sees the export-window message - capture between calls or assert on log output (found reviewing PR #4992). And the `run_timeout` watchdog is inert against blocking compute: `ComponentBase.start()` awaits `asyncio.wait_for(asyncio.shield(task), timeout=self.run_timeout)` on the component's **own** loop, which can only time out if that loop regains control — blocking compute never yields, so the watchdog never fires (GH#5075: `LoadMLComponent._do_training` calls the sync `train_curriculum()` with no offload). **Corrected 2026-09-22:** an earlier version of this entry said the components share the main event loop and a blocking call stalls shutdown, HA callbacks and every other component — wrong on both counts. Components are not tasks sharing one loop: `Components.start()` starts each one through `base.create_task()` (`hass.py`), which spawns a real non-daemon OS thread per component running its own `asyncio.run()` — so a sync CPU-bound call inside an async component method blocks **only that component's** loop; other components and the main loop keep running. The real cost is shutdown: `stop_all()` (`hass.py`) calls `terminate()` — which stops every component, logs each "Stopped" and "Predbat terminated" — and then `t.join(5 * 60)` per component thread; a component whose `run()` has not returned keeps its thread alive, so the join waits up to 5 minutes and after the timeout the interpreter's own non-daemon-thread shutdown waits again, without a timeout. That is why a #5075 log can show "Predbat terminated" with training still logging minutes later. The misleading "Lock released - event loop is free during training" comment is still in `load_ml_component.py` on main; PR #5112 (open draft, unmerged) fixes load_ml both ways — `asyncio.to_thread` plus a `stop_callback` on `LoadPredictor.train()`/`train_curriculum()` checked per epoch and per curriculum pass — and rewrites the comment. Note freeing the loop alone is **not** sufficient to make shutdown prompt: the thread only exits once `run()` returns, so an uninterruptible call holds the join whether it blocks the loop or a worker the loop is awaiting — a long-running component needs both off-the-loop and a check of `api_stop` inside the long call. Symptom pointer: restart/upgrade hangs for minutes, every component logged Stopped, "Predbat terminated" printed, process still alive → find the component whose `run()` cannot return, starting with any synchronous compute inside an `async def` (load_ml first). #3448 (fixed v8.33.8) was the run-blocking flavour, serialised against the prediction pools but never offloaded off the loop, which is why the shutdown flavour survives. The watchdog's timeout path is the complement of that shutdown flavour (found reviewing PR #5112, open at the time of writing): on timeout `start()` cancels the awaiting task — `asyncio.wait_for(asyncio.shield(task), timeout=self.run_timeout)` then `task.cancel()` (`component_base.py`) — which for a worker started with `await asyncio.to_thread(...)` raises `CancelledError` at the await and leaves the executor thread running to completion, a result nobody consumes; the timeout path never sets `api_stop`, the loop re-fires `run()` on the next tick with no guard against a second concurrent run, and the cancelled run never stamps its completion markers — so two workers can race on shared state and any cooperative-stop hook keyed solely on `api_stop` never trips on a watchdog timeout. The in-tree fix shape is a per-run cancellation handle fed by `stop()`, the timeout path and `fatal_error` (`component_base.py` already treats `fatal_error` as a stop condition). Only bites once a component moves CPU-bound work off the loop — unreachable for load_ml before that, because the synchronous training pinned the loop (GH#5075). Another startup-stall shape (GH#5134, code-read): a phase-0 component whose *constructor* fails (HAInterface raising on a 502 from `/api/services` during an HA restart) leaves the dependent components constructed but never `api_started` - their `run()` short-circuits while the HA component is missing - and `Components.start()` then blocks up to the full 10-minute `ComponentBase.wait_api_started()` default on each of them, so an HA core restart costs ~12.5 min of Predbat downtime instead of the ~20s restart loop. (`HAInterface` overrides the wait to 240s, but the failed instance never constructed, so that override is not the one biting; the startup backoff caps at 128 minutes and only the 10-min wait bounds a stalled phase.) On "Predbat down/slow after an HA restart", compare the log's phase-0 timestamps against the 60s→128min backoff gaps plus the 10-min expiry. One generic first-cycle trap from GH#5177: if `run()` raises or returns False while `first` is still True, `ComponentBase.start()` keeps `first` True (it is only cleared once `api_started` is set) and re-runs the whole first-cycle block on the 60s→128min startup backoff — so a component whose `health_message()` is tied to a short cooldown shows a blank health message for most of the gap between attempts, and the run status says only `component errors: <component name>` even though the component knows why; the pattern to copy is a diagnostic that stays set until a request succeeds (the Solis quota one did, and #5185 extended that to the datalogger cooldown). **The serial wait means one dead component costs 10 minutes of restart, and the count is the delay ÷ 10 (GH#5580, log arithmetic verified 2026-10-17):** `Components.start()` creates a component's task and blocks on `wait_api_started()` (10-minute timeout, `component_base.py`) *before creating the next task in the phase*, so a dead component eats its full window every restart and the dead-component count in the failing phase is the restart delay divided by 10 minutes (GH#5580: Kraken "failed to start" at 11:12:39, Axle the same at 11:22:39); a deterministic failure never sets `api_started`, so it consumes the window every time — E.ON Next began rejecting the email/password token mint with HTTP 400 around 16 Oct 2026 (GH#5580) — and `ComponentBase.start()`'s own ladder (60s doubling from the first retry, `backoff_interval`) keeps re-running the doomed attempt inside it. Fix shape logged on the issue (maintainer call pending): spawn all of a phase's tasks before waiting on any, bounding the wait by one timeout instead of N. Same arithmetic, opposite trigger to GH#5134 (a phase-0 constructor failure), so read them together on a slow-restart report. **An exception escaping run() is not a crash (GH#5584, code-read on main 2026-10-17):** `start()` wraps every `run()` call in a bare `except Exception` — the framework logs `Error:` + traceback, books `count_errors`/`non_fatal_error_occurred()` and retries one 60s cycle later, but **the remainder of that cycle is skipped** — anything after the raising call (publishing, wiring, further fetches) did not run — and on the `first`/startup path `api_started` is never set, so startup keeps backing off 60s→128min. Severity framing: for a component traceback report, the analysis is which steps after the raise got skipped and whether it can starve startup, not "the component crashed". And the catch-all is not total coverage: `json.JSONDecodeError` (a `ValueError`) slips through an `(aiohttp.ClientError, asyncio.TimeoutError)` except clause — kraken's own GraphQL path catches bare `ValueError` (`kraken.py`), and `gecloud.py`/`solax.py` catch `(aiohttp.ContentTypeError, json.JSONDecodeError)`, the in-repo precedents for an undecodable-body fetch. **Liveness trap: `update_success_timestamp()` on cached-data-only cycles (GH#5582).** A component whose run() stamps liveness from a cycle that only republished cache stayed healthy forever — the is-alive staleness gate never expired. The fix is an `is_alive()` override: enphase (`api_started and bool(self.sites)`) and, since PR #5583, Kraken (`api_started and not oauth_failed`) — both added after "nothing alerted me" reports; check the call sites on any component-health question for one whose run() refreshes off a cached artifact. **Empty-account convention (found reviewing the #5711 Hypervolt component pre-merge; the merged code now enacts it):** a cloud component's `run()` should return **True** when the account is signed in but holds zero devices — Wallbox's `poll()` is the precedent, and `hypervolt.py` does exactly this (`return True` with `update_success_timestamp()` left unstamped, so `api_started` is set but the 60-minute liveness check still reports the account empty) — not fall out of its no-fresh-data gate into `return False`, which never sets `api_started`: `Components.start()` blocks on `wait_api_started()` before starting the next component in the phase, so the serial-wait arithmetic (GH#5580) adds ~10 min to *every* Predbat restart and `count_errors` books every attempt. The dividing rule: returning False on missing *data* is correct liveness (the GH#5582 trap runs the other way); it is the zero-device account that must count as "started, empty". Review lesson: stub transports always carry chargers, so nothing pins the empty-account path — probe it explicitly in component reviews. | `components` |
| Run loop (`predbat.py`) | The HA-interface guard at the top of `update_time_loop()` and `run_time_loop()` (predbat.py, two near-identical copies) exists for two trigger conditions - `ha_interface is None`, and interface present with `websocket_active` False and `db_primary` False. PR #5137 (GH#5135) added the guard's two-branch wording and tested only the first, which is not the one production reaches: `ha.py` sets `websocket_active = False` on a websocket disconnect and `db_primary` defaults off, so the reachable trigger is a websocket drop on a db-primary-off install. `test_time_loop_guard.py` covers the None branch only, so a future edit to the guard's condition (dropping the `db_primary` term, swapping and/or) passes CI silently; a future "Predbat loop died / fatal_error loop" report starts there. The guard's contract lives in both copies and their log wording - a change to one copy alone looks correct and is half-wrong. | none |
| HA websocket socketLoop (`ha.py`, `hass.py`) | Event/select/number handlers run **on** the socketLoop coroutine: the socketLoop awaits `trigger_callback()` (`userinterface.py`) directly at its `call_service`-event sites (`ha.py`), so an update/save-restore handler whose `call_notify()` blocks waiting for a queued service call (`async_call_service_websocket_command()` waits on `event.wait(2 * 60)`, `ha.py`) deadlocks against the same loop that would service it — self-released only by that 2-minute timer. Ordinary notifications are safe (their threads are not wedged into the loop) and manual selects are safe (entity writes go via REST `set_state` POST, not the WS queue). `Hass.run_in_executor()` (`hass.py`) builds its ThreadPoolExecutor in a `with` and returns from inside the block, so the context-exit `shutdown(wait=True)` makes every call site block its loop thread for the whole callback. Discriminator for a "notification never arrived" report: `Warn: Service call X/Y failed or timed out: result {'response': None, 'success': None, ...}` with a delay of *exactly* 2:00 and **no** `Service call timeout for request id N` line means the queued command was never dequeued and sent — the timeout path sets `error="timeout"` and logs that line, so its absence plus an all-None holder is the never-sent signature; a routed result with present-but-null `success` is the different, non-deadlocked shape (commit `2ac3a355`). Symptom combo: notifications never arrive + `Warn: Web Socket closed` right after + `failed: connection_lost` on an unrelated write in the same window = HA dropped the WS connection server-side during the 2-minute stall, and the unrelated write died when the drop was detected at resume. GH#3460 (reopened 2026-10-21, reporter on v9.4.3) — verified by reading these symbols on main plus three timestamp pairs in the reporter's log; not reproduced by test. Posted fix direction: dispatch EVENT_LISTEN_LIST callbacks off the socketLoop coroutine, and/or make `run_in_executor()` non-blocking. | none |
| Carbon intensity (`carbon.py`, `fetch.py`) | The Carbon Intensity API (`https://api.carbonintensity.org.uk`) publishes only ~48h ahead and often far less - on 2026-09-06 it had nothing at all past end-of-day, national and regional alike, so an `fw48h` request from *now* returned 15h. Two consequences worth separating. (a) The API spec requires `{from}` as an ISO8601 `YYYY-MM-DDThh:mmZ` datetime; `fetch_carbon_data()` passed a bare `YYYY-MM-DD` and made *two* calls, the second from now+48h, which lands past the horizon and returns a bare `{}` with HTTP 200 - logged at `Error:` severity every fetch. That is upstream truncation surfacing as a Predbat error, not a broken integration - **fixed in PR #4958**: `fetch_carbon_data()` now makes one spec-format call built from `datetime.now(timezone.utc)` (`carbon.py:49-53`), which covers the whole published horizon, and the comment there records why UTC rather than a local date. (b) `carbon_intensity.get(minute, 0)` in `prediction.py:1279/1298` scores an uncovered minute as **zero** gCO2/kWh, so the tail of the plan looks carbon-free and `best_carbon` swings wildly between cycles (410kg vs 1365kg on consecutive runs). The zero-scoring itself is still in the code, re-verified on main; what mitigates it is `carbon_replicate()` (`fetch.py:1767`, alongside `rate_replicate`), which extends the forecast forward on the 24h cycle so fewer minutes fall through to the default. Check `metric_carbon` before calling this a planning bug - at 0p/kg it only distorts reporting. | `carbon` |
| Storage cache expiry (`storage.py`) | Expiry is judged by the **real clock, never Predbat's**: `load()` and `cleanup_expired()` compare the `.meta` sidecar's expiry against `datetime.now(timezone.utc)`, and the hourly cleanup **deletes** an expired file plus sidecar — so an entry whose expiry was built from `self.now_utc` is born expired once Predbat's clock and the real one drift apart (debug replay, paused HA, tests). Every `expiry=` call site on main derives from the real clock; `save_plan()` was the last exception and PR #5100 fixed it (GH#5079). This is the counterweight to the "build test dates from Predbat's clock" trap below — the discriminator is *which clock the code under test compares against*: slot/rate logic measures against `midnight_utc`/`now_utc`, storage expiry compares against the real one. Test tip from the same investigation: before chasing a (culprit, victim) pair from the fuzzer, run the victim alone **and at more than one time of day** — a test that builds its "expired" timestamp from the fixture clock brackets the day rather than failing outright (`plan_persistence` Test 5 was broken on its own). | `plan_persistence` |
| DB manager / DB engine (`db_manager.py`, `db_engine.py`) — opt-in path | `set_state_db()`'s "Convert time to GMT+0" line subtracts `utcoffset().seconds // 3600` hours, correct only for whole-hour non-negative offsets: a negative offset loses `timedelta.days` (24 h off — `timedelta(hours=-5)` normalises to `days=-1, seconds=68400`, so 19 h is subtracted instead of 5 h added) and a half-hour offset truncates (+05:30 stored as +05:00). Verified red→green through the db_manager test module (GH#5693, 2026-10-22); fix + regression test are draft PR #5698 (unmerged at the 2026-10-26 flush). `db_engine`'s read path lowercases the entity id while the write path does not, so a write of `sensor.X_case` then a `get_state_db("sensor.x_case")` returns `None` — and the index miss is `if not entity_index: return None`, which also treats a genuine index 0 as missing (test-observed; invisible in production, where entity ids are lowercase). The two write/read sites name the stamp differently: `set_state_db` returns it as `last_changed` (no `Z`) while a read-back arrives as `last_updated` with a trailing `Z` — index the read-back by its own key name and `.rstrip("Z")`, as the existing test does. `db_enable`/`db_primary`/`db_mirror_ha` all default `False`, so a db-bug impact claim applies to that opt-in population only. | `db_manager` |
| Manual override selects (`userinterface.py`) | The option list for a manual override select has **three independent builders**, each ending in the same `if values not in time_values ... item["options"] = ...` tail: `manual_times()` (the time-slot selects, `manual: True`), `manual_rates()` (the rate/value selects, `manual_rate: True`) and `api_select_update()` (`manual_api` only, `api: True`; `api_select()` is its read side). A change to how the lists look or are ordered must be made in all three or the dropdowns diverge — `manual_api` is easy to miss, it lives ~100 lines above the other two and is worded differently. `manual_select()` cannot drive `manual_api`: it branches on `manual_rate` and otherwise falls through to `manual_times()`, which would render an API-command select as time slots (GH#5105, verified by reading and by driving both paths from the PR #5108 test). Two incidental details: with nothing selected `values` is `""` and that tail appends an **empty-string option** (rendered as `None` on the web config page), and nothing indexes `options` positionally anywhere — selection matches the literal `"off"` — so a reorder is safe despite `impact()` rating all three methods HIGH (they sit on `initialize`/`update_time_loop`/`run_time_loop`; the risk is cosmetic). **`manual_rates()` prunes elapsed slots every cycle, which re-prices today's cost history at base (GH#5517, code-read on main 92478319, 2026-10-12):** the per-cycle rebuild (`manual_rates()` called from `fetch.py`'s config-options pass) drops every stored override slot starting before the current slot boundary and rewrites the selection without them (`expose_config(force=True)`), and the dropdown only ever offers slots from now forward — so an elapsed slot's cheap rate vanishes from `rate_import` and `today_cost()` (output.py) re-prices those minutes at base, a single step up at the boundary equal to the elapsed slot's kWh × base rate. A past slot chosen from the dropdown is silently discarded (it is filtered before the rewrite), so there is no way to correct a past slot's cost on the select path either. Evidence trap: because the stored selection is rewritten every cycle, **a debug yaml captured after a slot has passed never shows what the user originally selected** — ask the reporter what they set the dropdown to and when. Two open PRs already cover the family — #5078 (retain manual selections 24h past the slot, absolute dates, retroactive correction) and #5425 (persist effective rates through Storage, covering manual rates and IOG-dispatch disappearance alike, the open #2785 umbrella) — duplicate-check there before opening a new fix. | `manual_api` |
| Marginal rate sensors (`marginal.py`) | The `binary_sensor.<prefix>_marginal_rate_now_<load>_is_cheap`/`_is_moderate` flags are **plan-relative predictions, never live inverter state** (GH#5107): `calculate_marginal_costs()` injects +1/2/4/8 kWh over the next hour (`MARGINAL_TIME_OFFSETS`) into a fixed best-plan prediction and takes the raw `run_prediction()[0]` delta. Bands: cheap ≤ 1.2 × `rate_min`, moderate ≤ max(0.5 × `rate_max`, 1.5 × `rate_min`) — on a 6.9p/28.9p tariff that is a narrow 8.3p–14.4p band. The delta **excludes `compute_metric`'s battery_value and metric_keep terms**, so battery-served extra load is priced only via displaced future import/export — while the plan is overnight charge-and-export arbitrage an extra kWh costs the *export* rate in the model, so cells flip mid-band while the battery is visibly exporting, and the overnight export slots sit near the profitability threshold and enter/leave the plan between updates. The sensors only refresh at the `calculate_marginal_costs()` call site in `plan.py`'s publish path, so they can be up to `calculate_plan_every` (default 10 min) stale. Start here on any "marginal sensor looks wrong" report; Sigenergy EMS export outside the plan is invisible to them. **The band stats read event-contaminated values (GH#5382, dump-verified 2026-10-04):** the free/saving/Axle loaders run before the final rate scan, so with an Octopus free session in the horizon `rate_min` is 0, `cheap_threshold = rate_min × 1.2 = 0` makes `is_cheap = (cost <= 0)` permanently false (a marginal cell is never exactly 0 — extra load is modelled as displacing stored battery) and `moderate_threshold` swallows everything; while the sensor's own published `rate_min`/`rate_max` *attributes* come from `rate_min_base`/`rate_max_base` (`marginal.py`) — band and attributes disagree, and the attributes are the base-tariff shape the band should follow (fix direction: derive the band from the base values it already publishes). | none |
| Manual rates (`fetch.py` `basic_rates()`/`rate_replicate()`) | `day_of_week` rules are stamped across the **whole rate horizon**, each day against its own weekday (**fixed in PR #5173**, merged 2026-09-24, GH#5168 — previously only the explicitly modelled days were filtered and the day+2 extension write seeded whatever weekday day+2 actually was not, so the *last rule processed for a time-of-day slice won* beyond the modelled days and a weekend catch-all flattened the weekday peaks exactly 48h out, every later day then taking the previous day's pattern through `rate_replicate()`, which has no `day_of_week` notion; start there on any "next-but-one day's manual rates are wrong" report). Other pre-#5173 shapes: a midnight-spanning weekday rule never reached day+2's early hours (the wrapped half seeded day+3 instead), and yesterday was checked against *today's* weekday (`int(minute_index / 1440)` truncates negative fractions to 0). Keep the mechanism for pre-#5173 logs. A consequence worth knowing before calling anything a bug on a current build: a fully-tiled manual tariff is never `rate_replicate()`d any more, so `metric_future_rate_offset_*`, `futurerate_adjust_*` and the replicated plan markers no longer touch manual-tariff minutes — "the offset stopped applying" on a manual tariff is this. **Still live — two traps that survive the fix.** A rule ending `:59:59` leaves the **final minute of every window unwritten**: `time_string_to_stamp()` floors `06:59:59` to minute 419 and the window is `range(start, 419)`, so today that minute keeps `basic_rates()`' pre-init 0 (`for minute in range(24*60): rates[minute] = 0`) and `rate_replicate()` copies it forward (probe-verified: 0p at 06:59/08:59/16:59/20:59/23:59 each day, `rate_minmax()` min 0); symptom `Import rates: min 0` / `Export rates: min 0` from a manual tariff with no genuine zero rate; workaround: end each rule at the next rule's start (`07:00:00`); in tests, assert window interiors, not exact whole-range equality. What the planner does with a lone 0p import minute is unverified (raised on PR #5173's review thread, not fixed there). And a minute left unmatched by the day filter falls to `rate_replicate()`, which applies `metric_future_rate_offset_import`/`_export`, so a test that only matches the tariff's own rates must pin that offset to 0. The fix's tests (13/14 in `test_basic_rates.py`, plus third-day assertions in 6/7) pin `day_of_week` on every horizon day in either rule order — before #5173 they asserted only today and tomorrow, so keep the lesson: a `day_of_week` regression can land green against tests that never look past tomorrow. | `basic_rates` |
| Manual load adjust (`fetch.py` `step_data_history()`) | `select.predbat_manual_load_adjust` is **additive**, never a replacement (GH#5176): `step_data_history()` adds `load_adjust.get(minute_absolute, 0) * step / plan_interval_minutes` on top of the ML/historical load (floored so total load cannot go below zero), and `output.py` mirrors it for the published `load_energy_predicted`. The docs disagreement that misled the reporter ("overwrite predicted load" in docs/manual-api.md vs "adjustments" in customisation.md) was fixed by the maintainer (`c06bff91`, 2026-09-23 — docs now say "add the adjustment amount (which can be positive or negative)"), though the plan-UI cell still says only "Adjust load (kWh)" with no sign hint. Triage method: the reporter's plan HTML marks overridden slots with the `±` symbol — that marker plus the neighbouring un-marked slots attributes "applied but additive" vs "genuinely ignored" in one glance; a +0.2 on top of a multi-kWh ML slot is invisible in the SoC drop, which is the signature of "user expected replacement semantics". `load_today_comparison` (registered) asserts the additive behaviour (+1.0 → predicted load +1.0). Recurring upstream cause: free-event load baked into history — ML training has no free-event exclusion and `load_scaling_free` only scales *forecast* event slots (GH#4942 open). The select's horizon is `MANUAL_RATE_MAX_MINUTES` (7 days, `const.py`), so same-day overrides cannot be dropped by the horizon gate. Suspected, not verified: a small (~0.1-0.2 kWh/slot) systematic offset between ML sensor values and the plan's displayed load on non-overridden slots — do not do per-slot arithmetic beyond the clean case without re-deriving it. | `load_today_comparison` |
| Status health (`output.py`, `predbat.py`) | False `Status: <mode> (unhealthy)` on the web dashboard (GH#5181, **still live on main**, re-verified 2026-09-26): `predbat.status`'s `last_updated` attribute is written with `now_utc_real.strftime(TIME_FORMAT)` (`output.py:2699`; `TIME_FORMAT` is `%z`, which never emits a colon on any Python) but `is_running()` parses it with `datetime.fromisoformat()` (`predbat.py:1960`), which is colon-strict before Python 3.11 — on ≤3.10 every parse raises `ValueError` → `return False` → `web.py` renders "(unhealthy)" and the API ping returns 500. On 3.11+ the relaxed parser accepts `+0100`, which is why CI on 3.14 stays green. `load_plan()` is **not** affected: its writer uses `.isoformat()` (always colon form), so that round-trip works pre-3.11 too. Trap for the obvious `str2time()` swap: it would lose the naive-string tolerance `is_running()` deliberately keeps (an install upgraded from before the timezone-aware write persists a naive `last_updated`; `web.py:988` already parses this attribute with `str2time()` — in-tree precedent but naive-intolerant) — fix the writer to `.isoformat()` or keep the naive branch. Suspected, not verified: which shipped environments actually run ≤3.10 (no addon/Dockerfile in-repo to read); no test can settle it (Python-version dependent). | none |
| Futurerate / AgilePredict (`futurerate.py`, `fetch.py`) | **Who fills missing minutes from what is decided by `rate_replicate(..., use_future=...)` (fetch.py; PR #5738 merged 2026-10-25):** `use_future=True` takes a missing minute from `future_energy_rates_import/export` (marking it copied, so next-day copy+offset is bypassed) — the live import/export replication and `rate_base_min_max` pass True; default False for gas replication, annual replay and compare rows that are not the live tariff. Consequence across the tree: `rate_import_no_io`, `rate_import_base` and `rate_min_base`/`rate_max_base` **now include the forecast fills**, so compare recognition (`is_live_published()`, compare.py — dp2 equality over minutes ≥ now in both tables), the IOG strip value (`dynamic_load_car_strip_feed_rates()`'s `rate_max_base`) and the stored-energy-value cap (plan.py) all read forecast-including tables — a "compare stopped recognising my tariff after enabling a futurerate setting" report starts there. For existing Nord Pool users day-2+ fills use each day's own prediction instead of a copy of day 1's (tag still `"future"`). AgilePredict API facts (live-probed 2026-10-25): `GET https://prices.fly.dev/api/{REGION}/?days=N&high_low=false`, REGION ∈ `ABCDEFGHJKLMNP` (I and O excluded), N ≤ 14, returns a **list** of one region object whose `prices` are half-hour `{date_time, agile_pred}` entries — `&export=true` returns the export forecast under the same field names; timestamps are **mixed within one payload** (`+01:00` for today's tail, `Z` for later days across a DST changeover — convert with aware datetime objects, `replace("Z", "+00:00")` first); and the forecast **starts at the current half hour, not midnight**, so a fresh payload has no coverage for today's past minutes — "the forecast fills today's morning" is a conversion bug, not the API. AgilePredict predictions are cached ~180 min (PR #5792, merged 2026-10-27 — 4 fetches/day). Constrained-fit facts (PR #5731, open at writing, hand-worked not executed): `futurerate_calibrate` fits mult/all-range adders/peak adders against real Agile rates in formula space, then the publish transform undoes the fit, so **published ≈ real within the fitted window whatever the vat/discount constants are** — the constants' only effect is the projection beyond the fetched window (`fetch.py` substitutes future rates for minutes past the published window), and no offline test can guard them (`test_futurerate_auto.py` mocks the calibrator out; `test_nordpool.py`'s Agile-vs-Nordpool comparison is deliberately loose — max diff 20 p import / 10 p export, `test_nordpool.py` — so both old and new constants pass it). Root cause of the ticket: the 2026 Agile formula shift (flat ex-VAT discount + 0% VAT) needs a flat adder outside `all_range`'s window, so the fit clamps and publishes ≈+2.3p high on all future minutes — any future flat formula change outside that window reproduces the same silent clamp-and-skew; the general fix shape is transforming real data back into original-formula space before fitting. | `futurerate` |

## Symptom → first place to look

| Report | Start here |
|--------|-----------|
| `Failed to create inverter 0: argument of type 'int' is not iterable` (or the 3.11+ wording `'int' is not a container or iterable`), then `Failed to fetch inverter data, not able to compute a plan` | A literal numeric `reserve:` in apps.yaml, e.g. `reserve: - 1`, instead of an entity id — common on inverter types with no reserve entity (reported on Huawei). `reserve_device_bounds()` (PR #4956, so v8.55.1+ only; v8.55.0 is fine) resolves the value and passes it straight to `get_state_wrapper()` as an entity id, which evaluates `"$" in entity_id` (`predbat.py:190`) unguarded and raises `TypeError`. **Fixed in PR #5005 (v9.0.2)** for the whole class, not just this call site: `get_state_wrapper()` and `set_state_wrapper()` now log `Warn: ... is a fixed value, not an entity id` and return the default / `False` instead of raising, so current main degrades the literal to an unread setting rather than crashing. Keep the crash signature for pre-v9.0.2 versions, where the bisect still reads v8.55.0 works, v8.55.1/v9.0.0/v9.0.1 fail with the same apps.yaml. Workaround (still the right one at any version): delete the `reserve:` line or point it at an entity — lossless on `has_reserve_soc: False` types, where the literal only feeds `reserve_percent_current = max(literal, battery_min_soc)` and a value at or below `battery_min_soc` (default 4) is inert. The same fault arrives in two shapes (GH#5033, GE/GivTCP + Solis): the main create path wraps `Inverter()` in try/except (`execute.py`) and logs `Failed to create inverter N: ...`, but `balance_inverters()` re-creates every inverter each cycle with `Inverter(self, id, quiet=True)` and **no try/except**, so on a multi-inverter setup the TypeError propagates through `timer_tick` as a bare traceback through `balance_inverters → Inverter __init__ → reserve_device_bounds → get_state_wrapper` — and it re-crashes every cycle even while the main path reports cleanly. A report showing a timer_tick traceback with `balance_inverters` in the stack is the same apps.yaml fault (pre-v9.0.2; the #5005 guard removes the crash source from both paths). Since PR #5149 (merged 2026-09-19) there is no unguarded balance-path construction at all: `balance_inverters()` is a pure function in `utils.py` driven from `rebalance_inverter_rates()` on the inverter poll (SoC balancing defaults off, `balance_inverters_enable`), and the single `Inverter(self, id)` construction site in `execute.py` is itself wrapped in try/except — so a bare traceback through a balance path is pre-#5149 only. |
| `Error: You have not set load_today or load_forecast in apps.yaml, you will have no load data` then `Error: Exception raised ValueError` every cycle, and the plan/SoC stop updating | `fetch_sensor_data()` raises a bare `raise ValueError` (fetch.py) when neither arg resolves and nothing in the fetch path catches it — the raise unwinds the whole cycle from the fetch call site (`predbat.py`, `sensor_force_replan = self.fetch_sensor_data()`), so inverter reads, the plan and every sensor publish are skipped, and the generic `Error: Exception raised` handler catches it (GH#5092). The message and the `Error: load_today not set correctly` status both point at apps.yaml, but cloud components set `load_today` at runtime (Solis `automatic_config()`, GE Cloud `async_automatic_config()`, `sunsynk_const.py` maps it too), so during a component startup delay there is nothing in apps.yaml to fix — this is the fetch-side end of the Solis startup-outage family (see the Solis row for why the delay happens; check the Components panel for startup backoff before telling the user to edit apps.yaml). No graceful fallback exists: `self.load_minutes` persists in memory only once a previous fetch has succeeded, so a restart into a not-yet-configured component has nothing to fall back on; any fix has to decide what the planner runs on for that cycle (maintainer call on #5092). |
| `Error: timer_tick caught exception: <X>` in the log with `predbat.status` saying `Error: Exception raised <X>` (often with a printed traceback just above) — "Predbat stopped planning" but no crash | An exception escaped `update_pred()` mid-fetch: the scheduled-trigger handler records the status and re-raises (`predbat.py`), then `timer_tick` catches it (`hass.py`). The app survives, but the aborted cycle computed no plan and executed nothing — find the raising function from the exception's own payload. `read timeout=20` in the exception text → `download_octopus_rates_func` (octopus.py, the 20 s `requests.get`); `read timeout=120` → `download_octopus_free_func` (octopus.py, 120 s) — the only two requests-based download paths that catch `ConnectionError` alone, and `ReadTimeout` is a *sibling*, not a subclass, of `ConnectionError` (only `ConnectTimeout` is both), so read-timeouts escape while connect-timeouts are handled (GH#5743; fix drafted in PR #5801). Exhaustiveness checked 2026-10-25: `ha.py`'s `api_call()` handles `Timeout`/`ReadTimeout`/`ConnectionError`/`JSONDecodeError` and `futurerate.py` wraps its request in `except Exception`, so any *new* `except requests.exceptions.*` narrower than those around a bounded `requests.get` is a candidate for the same escape. |
| A startup burst of `Warn: Failed to decode response <Response [404]> from http://<ha>/api/history/period/...` then `Error: Too many API errors, stopping`, `Error: Web Socket failed to reconnect, stopping....`, `Info: HA interface stopped` — and Predbat then sits "stopped but running", web answering `/api/ping` 500 | The REST-layer **consecutive** error counter in `HAInterface.api_call()` (`ha.py`), not the websocket: a non-JSON response — which is what HA's 404 page surfaces as (`JSONDecodeError`) — plus every timeout or connection error increments `api_errors`, it resets **only on a fully successful call**, has no time decay, and at 10 calls `fatal_error_occurred()` (deliberate design since #2110 as a recovery mechanism). The websocket lines are the consequence, not the cause — the socket loop exits on `fatal_error`, waits 5s to reconnect, hits the outer break and logs the two stopping lines — so a websocket that was healthy all along is dragged down by REST-only 404s on the history endpoint. The threshold is reachable within one startup fetch: `get_history()` fetches each window in `HISTORY_CHUNK_DAYS` = 3-day chunks with nothing marking a chunk handled, so an entity's window is `ceil(max_days_previous/3)` consecutive calls (`days_previous` defaults `7` → 8 days → 3 calls per entity — `days_previous: 28+` reaches the 10 from one entity), and the boot-time full fetch is scheduled immediately at init (`update_time_loop` first fire `datetime.now()`, `predbat.py`), so a few entities' 404s back to back reach 10 in seconds. A non-2xx response **with a JSON body does not count** — it parses and is treated as data (degenerate, untested). While `fatal_error` is set `is_running()` reads False so `/api/ping` answers 500 while the web component keeps serving; standalone/Docker `stop_all()` still exits code 0 (per-thread join bounded at 5 min), so an `on-failure` supervisor policy does not restart it and the ping can answer 500 for minutes during shutdown — the official add-on wrapper restarts after ~20s. The fix is a maintainer design call in the counter (per-endpoint counting, or retry/backoff on history fetches) plus new tests, since existing tests assert the current behaviour. Related, not duplicate: #5134 (failed interface init blocks 10 min in `wait_api_started` — opposite mechanism, same "HA not ready at startup" family). **One HA outage can walk *both* mechanisms in sequence (GH#5437, 2026-10-08):** after the counter fires, a log can continue into #5134's mechanism — `Cannot initialise Home Assistant Interface` (the constructor's bare `raise ValueError` at the `ha_key`/direct-connect checks makes `str(e)` empty, hence the trailing comma with nothing after it) → repeating `HAHistory: No HAInterface available` spaced on the component startup backoff (starts 60 s, doubling — `component_base.py`; `wait_api_started()` gives up after 10 min) → the `HA interface not found` raise in `update_pred()` → wrapper restart. So a log showing that full pattern maps to both open tickets at once — don't force-close into either. `fatal_error` is initialised False once and never cleared in-process: only the web Restart button and the standalone runtime loop act on it, `Components.initialize()` runs once per process, so one interface means one attempt per process. The delta to probe before theorising: ask for the FULL predbat.log — repeated `Predbat: Startup` banners mean the restart loop is alive but not converging, no banner after the fatal lines means the process sat stopped-but-running and the wrapper never restarted it (both known mechanisms end in add-on auto-recovery: ~12.5 min in #5134's log, ~20-30 s cycles otherwise). Attribution note (GH#5580): a 502 flood against `http://supervisor/core/api/websocket` (`WSServerHandshakeError`) alongside `HA interface not found` means HA core itself was restarting — the HA `2026.10.2` update outage in that log — not any integration or Predbat component, so don't chase it through this row's counter. **The serial wait after a fatal latch is a pure sleep (GH#5744, code-read 2026-10-25):** `wait_api_started()` (`component_base.py`) polls only `api_started` — no `fatal_error`/`api_stop` check, even though `ComponentBase.start()`'s run loop exits on both — so after a fatal latch each remaining component in the phase waits its full 600 s with nothing to wait for, and `fatal_error` is never reset in-process (`predbat.py`), so the post-hang app stays stopped until a manual restart. Route a "Predbat slow/down after an HA restart" report three ways: a serial-wait hang (component failed-to-start lines 10 min apart) → open **#5134** (delay ÷ 10 min = dead components in the failing phase; the spawn-all-then-single-bounded-wait shape logged on #5580 fixes the serial cost); the `Too many API errors` latch itself (10 *consecutive* failures, incremented with no sleep, reset only by a successful call — so the counter never decays during a long outage and a *running* Predbat crosses it over successive ticks) → open **#5354**; 502-during-startup recovery → open **#5437**. Caution before tightening the 600 s timeout: `Components.start()`'s own startup backoff (retries at ~0/60/180/360 s) is what the wait races — a 120 s timeout marks failed a component that today starts on its third retry. And "the add-on restarts on exit(0)" rests on #5134's log plus the row above, not on wrapper source: the wrapper lives in the separate `springfall2008/predbat_addon` repo (out of reach from triage sessions — verify addon-side behaviour from the banner pattern, not repo access). |
| `predbat.log` shows nothing but repeating `Loading secrets from ...` / `Loading apps.yaml` pairs at a ~20 s cadence and never reaches the `Predbat: Startup` banner | Bootstrap apps.yaml parse failure: an unknown YAML tag (a typo'd or invented `!secret`-style key - only `!secret` has a constructor registered on the SafeLoader, `load_apps_yaml()` in utils.py) raises `ConstructorError`, caught in `Hass.__init__` (`hass.py`) which does bare `print(exc)` + `sys.exit(1)` - so the real two-line error is on the **add-on console log only** and can never appear in predbat.log; its emptiness apart from the loading pairs is the tell (GH#5530, reproduced with PyYAML). The ~20 s retry is the add-on wrapper restarting on exit, the same family as the row above rather than a Predbat loop. Triage disposition on #5530: enhancement - route the error through `self.log` and hint `did you mean !secret <name>?`; "continue loading" is not achievable since apps.yaml is the boot config. |
| `Warn: Unable to load the load forecast ... predheat.heat_energy$external` every cycle, startup validation `Invalid value None in element predheat.heat_energy$external`, and no `Predheat:` log lines despite the enable switch being on | Predheat's settings are not where the loader looks. `predheat_enable` is a Predbat UI switch, but the `predheat:` config domain must be nested **inside `pred_bat:`** — a top-level `predheat:` app section is silently discarded by the standalone loader, which reads only `config["pred_bat"]` (`hass.py:322`), and under AppDaemon a top-level section cannot load the bundled class at all. The whole `templates/predheat.yaml` is nested under `predbat:`; `docs/predheat.md` says "append it to the end of apps.yaml", which invites the mistake. `self.args.get("predheat", {})` then stays falsy so `PredHeat` is never instantiated (`predbat.py:2009`), the `predheat.heat_energy` entity is never published, and `fetch_extra_load_forecast` warns every cycle. Applies to any bundled sub-app configured by domain (the domain read is `self.args.get(domain, {}).get(arg, default)`) (GH#5002). |
| `cost_today` / `cost_today_import` steps up after each half hour of an Octopus Intelligent car charge and falls back 15-60 minutes later (GH#5520) | Not the plan and not the tariff: `today_cost()` (`output.py`) re-prices every elapsed minute of the day from the current `rate_import`, which is rebuilt each cycle, so anything that stops stamping an *elapsed* minute cheap shows at once. A planned dispatch only covers what is still to come (`trim_started_dispatch()` advances a running one's start; an ended one is dropped) and since #4114 is never promoted to completed, while `completedDispatches` lists it 15-60 minutes later. Check the log for the `Intelligent slot at` start moving forward each poll. The gap is bridged in `octopus_held_update()` / `octopus_held_dispatches()` (`octopus.py`): a half hour seen running, and not cancelled by `octopus_intelligent_dynamic`, is injected as a finished dispatch for `OCTOPUS_DISPATCH_HOLD_MINUTES`. The held record used to be memory-only: pre-#5581 a restart inside the window still showed the old step; since PR #5581 (2026-10-17) the held half hours are saved to Storage when they change and restored at startup. Test: `octopus_dispatch_hold`. Same re-pricing mechanism, different trigger: #5517 (manual rate overrides). |
| Plan tab blank / no plan shown in HA | `predbat.cost_today` missing from HA recorder history — a recorder configuration problem (GH#3936). Test: `faq_recorder_config`, and see `docs/faq.md`. |
| Exports happen at the wrong time | `plan.py` export optimisation order, plus `combine_export_slots` and `metric_min_improvement_export` in their config. |
| IOG: battery flat inside an evening dispatch that runs on into 23:30, plan waits until 01:00 to charge | Two causes, both **fixed on main in PR #5304 (merged 2026-09-30, v9.3.3)** - keep the mechanisms for pre-#5304 logs. (1) Dispatches applied by `rate_add_io_slots()` (Predbat's own Octopus component) never set `io_adjusted`, so the PV10 "slot goes away" re-pricing never fired; it now flags the minutes it lowers, matching BottlecapDave's `adjust_intelligent_rates()`, which flags only rates it lowered - never the fixed off-peak or over-cap time; and from PR #5392 (v9.3.6) "lowered" means by more than `IO_RATE_TOLERANCE` (a penny), so sub-penny dp2-rounding differences no longer mark the fixed off-peak window as adjusted or re-price it at the dispatch price. (2) With `car_charging_from_battery: false` the PV10 case still held the battery for the car in a vanished dispatch, so an early top-up had no value; PV10 now keeps the car at the dispatch rate but off the battery and releases the hold (kernel parity 15). Pre-fix check: `io_adjusted: {}` in the dump alongside live `octopus_slots`. The debug replay reuses the dump's `rate_import`/`io_adjusted` and never re-runs `rate_add_io_slots()`, even with `--redo`, so it cannot show a rate-overlay change - re-apply the overlay from `rate_import_no_io` in a wrapper. Also: with only the first (trusted) half hour left before 23:30 the benefit is under `metric_min_improvement_plan` (2p), so a dump taken late in the dispatch rightly shows no change. |
| IOG: the house battery charges at the back of (or not at all during) a long smart-charge dispatch whose rate is equally cheap throughout | Within-block placement is **metric-neutral** — the cost model has no term preferring the earlier minutes of an equally-priced dispatch (GH#5330, probe-verified on the reporter's own replay at main 57ec7bf1: charged-early 10:00–11:30 = 148.5602p, continuous 10:00–12:00 = 148.5586p, the reporter's 10:30 late ramp = 148.5745p — equal to rounding), so placement is decided by search convergence plus plan retention (`metric_min_improvement_plan`) — **second live instance GH#5344** (GE Hybrid + one car on IOG, 2026-10-02, reporter-log-verified): the mid-band charge limit flapped between equally-scored shapes tick to tick and the overnight limit shrank toward a later cap (91% at 00:05 → 99% by 04:45 → 100%) as the horizon narrowed, which is the tie surface shrinking, not an execute fault; concurrent battery charging during the car's dispatch is allowed and executed on this shape, so do not read a car dispatch as a charge window blocker; the IOG earlier-charge skew that *does* exist (`IO_ADJUST_SLOPE`/`_PIVOT`/`_MAX_DISCOUNT`/`_MAX_PENALTY`/`_DISCOUNT_HORIZON`, `_io_rate_adjustment()` inside `sort_window_by_price_combined()`, plan.py) is ranking-level only — a signed gradient over `io_adjusted`-flagged window starts, reordering candidate windows, never a tie-break in the metric — so equal-cost placements land wherever the search converged. **Since PR #5635 (merged 2026-10-19) that is no longer quite true while a dispatch run is confirmed in hand:** `io_confirmed_credit_update()` adds a score-only "bird in hand" credit to the dispatch-made-cheap run in force — 0.1p per kWh for the current half hour, divided by 1+n for the nth after it — taken off `metric_keep` in both engines with cost untouched (kernel ABI 8), so charging *early inside a confirmed cheap run* now scores slightly better than waiting, and the first replan after a run becomes boosted adopts it one-shot; the credit is smaller than `metric_min_improvement_plan`, so genuine cost differences still win. Suspected, not verified: PR #5304's PV10 "dispatch gone" hedge can *systematically* steer the planner to the un-flagged tail of a dispatch (the integration's `io_adjusted` flags can stop short of the dispatch end); and which search/retention path resolves equal-metric placements late. Cheap A/B technique: replay the dump, then compare direct `run_prediction()` variants — any placement fix must survive metric evaluation (a real cost term, or an explicit equal-metric tie-break that slides windows earlier inside an `io_adjusted` run). See also the previous row — pre-#5304 the dispatch minutes also were never `io_adjusted`-flagged, so the PV10 hedge did not even see them. |
| IOG: the house battery charges cheaply for the first few minutes of a car dispatch that started the moment it was published, then the plan reverts | The car-need gate from PR #5403 (v9.3.6, for #4482) can only withhold dispatch minutes from the half hour **after** the current one — `octopus_surplus_from()` returns `(minutes_now // 30 + 1) * 30` (octopus.py; the docstring states the carve-out as deliberate: the car may already have charged in the current one), and `rate_add_io_slots()` skips only `minute in self.octopus_surplus` — so a same-instant-start dispatch still cheap-stamps the current half hour even with `octopus_intelligent_consider_full` on (GH#5424, diffed the published v9.3.5 tarball against main). The only veto inside that half hour is the dynamic-load evidence path — 3 min start + 2 min sensor grace (`DYNAMIC_LOAD_CAR_START_MINUTES`/`DYNAMIC_LOAD_CAR_SENSOR_MINUTES`, const.py) then replan + strip — and with `octopus_intelligent_trust_slots` off the running decision `dynamic_load_car_cancelled.get(car_n, default_cancelled)` (plan.py) can retain a stale not-cancelled from a dormant (slot-less) phase, so a trust-off user's first cycle inside a brand-new dispatch is still trusted until the grace evidence lands. The car-side skip (`Car 0 is already charged, ignoring additional charging slot`, execute.py) does not stop the house plan. So "upgrade fixes it" is not an answer to a same-instant-start dispatch report without checking which half hour the dispatch started in. Overnight dispatches inside the fixed 23:30–05:30 band are tariff-cheap regardless (`in_iog_fixed_window` excludes them from the gate) — don't attribute overnight dispatch-stamp plans to this class; #4516 is the open umbrella for the residual. |
| Plan won't charge or export inside Intelligent dispatch windows while the same cheap price from manual rates cycles normally - and `pv_metric10_weight` alone turns the behaviour on/off | The PV10 scenario re-prices every `io_adjusted`-flagged minute past the first 30 simulated minutes at `max(rate_max, import_rate)` (the `dispatch_gone` rule, prediction.py mirrored in prediction_kernel.cpp; deliberate since PR #5304 fixing GH#5392, pinned by `test_dispatch_gone_priced_at_tariff_max` and the kernel-parity cases). The flags are set by the rate feed wherever a fetch carries an `adjust_key` (`fetch.py`, `is_intelligent_adjusted`), with no `octopus_intelligent_*` config gate; a manual rate over a flagged slot keeps the flag (`apply_manual_rates()`); the only per-minute deleter is `dynamic_load_car_strip_feed_rates()` - so PV10 can worst-case a dispatch minute Predbat itself stamped. `plan.py` weights the PV10 plan by `pv_metric10_weight` (default 0.15): one setting carries both solar pessimism and dispatch-withdrawal risk (GH#5540). Diagnostic: `io_adjusted` present around the window in the debug yaml. Fix trap: Python reads `io_adjusted` while the C kernel sees `io_flag` (`prediction_kernel.py`), so any switch must gate both in lockstep or `test_kernel_parity` fails. |
| "Random" exports that lose money on a flat export tariff | `metric_battery_value_scaling` below 1.0, then `metric_cloud_enable` being off. See "Check configuration before code" above (GH#4840) — the protective margin is ~1%, so a small reduction is enough. |
| Exports at the outgoing-Agile peak then imports through the rest of the evening at a higher rate — "it never sells above my buy price, so it is not optimised for cost" | Working as designed: the governing comparison is the sell price vs the **refill** price (overnight IOG band), not vs the simultaneous import price (GH#5509, dump-read + replay-denied arithmetic, 2026-10-12): selling ~12 kWh at ~25.6p and refilling ~17 kWh at ~3.8p nets ~+£2.3/day, and the pennies-scale loss is only the last stretch below the SoC floor. The reporter had also zeroed the overnight outgoing rate via their own `rates_export_override`, which strands the surplus at 0p and makes the daytime export look worse. The knob is `best_soc_keep` (soft, kWh). Reading the plan table card: the second/third columns are import/export rates, not SoC — cross-check a window's `average` against the rate column before reading the rows (the same misread cost GH#5509 its first pass). Distinct from #5205 (a midnight-split charge slot re-splitting into charge-export-charge — a genuine slot-splitting bug) and #4869 (negative prices). Second confirming report GH#5566 (Fox KH7 + IOG import + ~29p export band → 7.62p refill, 2026-10-16, plan/log-parsed): the family is neither Agile-outgoing- nor Sigenergy-specific, and the planner **cannot express "export hard but stop at 45%"** — every export-window target rung is `calc_percent_limit(self.best_soc_min, self.soc_max)` (plan.py; `optimise_export()` sweeps only window start plus IDLE/FREEZE/low-power modes), so a planned export discharges at full power and the stop line is the window length. The limit % a plan row displays is the **achieved** SoC, not a chosen level — `clip_export_slots()` raises the recorded target to the SoC the simulation reached (the `best_soc_min` target minus a 10-minute discharge margin) when it never got there — so don't read "34% limit" off a plan row as a planner decision; `set_export_low_power`'s rungs `[0.7, 0.5, 0.3]` (`LOW_EXPORT_POWER_LEVELS`) are the granularity lever, and `best_soc_keep` is the right knob for the tail because the penalty is **import-rate-weighted** (prediction.py) — it bears hardest exactly in peak-drain reports; `set_reserve_min` is the harder, round-the-clock floor. Refill-price governs the arithmetic: sell at the export band, refill at the cheap band, with only the pennies-scale tail below the keep-point losing — quoting that split settles the report. **Third instance of the family, GH#5579 (2026-10-17, code-read on main — all three sites re-verified 2026-10-18):** the recurring "don't let exports run the battery down to the true reserve; keep N% back during export windows" ask maps to `best_soc_min` (kWh, expert mode, default 0 — no config item named "export reserve" exists), and it binds **only inside planned export windows**, at three layers: the model floor `discharge_min = max(soc_max × export_limit%, reserve, best_soc_min)` (prediction.py), the export-window candidate target rungs (`calc_percent_limit(best_soc_min, soc_max)` in `optimise_export()`, plan.py) and the execute stop level `max(target, reserve, best_soc_min)` (execute.py) — outside export windows the floor is the reserve alone. Two nuances before a fix conversation: units are kWh (`soc_max × 25%` for a "25%" ask), and the plan writes its own export target register during the window — `export_target_soc_percent()` raises the written target to `max(reserve, best_soc_min)` only when `set_reserve_enable` is off (PR #4685's rule: where Predbat owns the reserve register the reserve enforces the floor and the target is left alone) — so "plan at 25% but leave the portal target at 20%" is not a mode that exists; the 20% floor lives in the reserve register outside windows. The maintainer's remedy on #5566 itself was raising `load_scaling90`'s default 0.7 → 0.9 (PR #5577, 2026-10-17) — the PV90 scenario's 15% weight now hedges a heavier load too. |
| Battery charges or freezes when it shouldn't | Freeze paths in `prediction.py` and the inverter component's control write; check `best_soc_keep` and the freeze-related config in the replay. Rule the plan out first: the signature of a *planned* freeze is a charge window whose limit equals the reserve percent, which `prediction.py` turns into a freeze and then blocks discharge across for the whole window, with execute writing `charge_limit` = current SoC and a 0 W discharge rate. All three together mean the planner chose it and the inverter did as it was told - on a tariff where every cycle loses money and stored energy is credited at replacement cost, holding the battery full and importing everything is the arithmetic working, not a control failure (GH#4890). |
| Battery pinned at 100% while the house imports at peak | Rule the plan out from the log before opening `plan.py`. `Completed run status <mode>` says what Predbat asked for and `Inverter N count register writes` says whether it changed anything: `Demand` on every cycle, zero writes, and a flat SoC together mean Predbat asked for discharge and something outside it refused - look at the reserve/min-SoC entity binding for that integration. Three successive analyses of GH#4961 theorised about a charge window spanning the peak that the reporter's own log shows never existed. |
| Weather alert active and the battery sits at its current SoC through the alert while the house imports at peak ("my keep: N% is ignored") | The alert keep is a **floor, not a hold** (GH#3200, replay-verified on main, 2026-10-23 — see the keep mechanisms bullet in Traps): it never raises the reserve and never stops discharge above keep%, so the pinned battery is the optimiser's cheapest defence of the floor. With `set_discharge_during_charge: false` that defence is a daytime charge window whose target equals the reserve value; with ddC true (default) the plan targets ≈ keep% as documented. The alert's own `keep:` value is set in the `alerts:` apps.yaml config (default 100 when omitted) — check it before calling the keep "ignored". Two overlapping alerts of the same storm both apply (the plan's ⚠ marker spans the union). |
| "How do I feed prices from integration X" / rates read zero or `Warn: Octopus: No Octopus data in sensor ... attribute 'rates' / 'raw_today' / 'prices'` on a third-party spot-price sensor | `fetch_octopus_rates()` (`octopus.py`) reads today's prices **only from an attribute named `rates`, `raw_today` or `prices`** — `raw_tomorrow` is added for a non-Octopus entity (name without `_current_rate`, the Nordpool shape) — so any other attribute name needs a HA template sensor re-emitting `raw_today`/`raw_tomorrow` (docs/energy-rates.md), then `metric_octopus_import`. Entry keys auto-detect in the order `rate` → `value_inc_vat`/`valid_from`/`valid_to` → `start`/`end` (with **scale ×100**, so EUR/kWh lands as cents-of-currency — tell non-UK reporters displayed costs read as minor units; plan decisions are unaffected) → `value` → `price`/`from`/`till`; none matching gives that Warn. Missing tomorrow is fine (`rate_replicate()` carries today forward) and 15-minute spot pricing wants `plan_interval_minutes: 15`. (GH#5676; code-read on main, 2026-10-24.) |
| Battery charges to 100% at Predbat start, on a `pred_bat_mode` change or on a `set_read_only` change (an inverter whose target register carries a floor, e.g. Victron ESS via `inverter_type: SK` or any profile with `has_charge_enable_time: False`) | `PredBat.reset_inverter()` (`execute.py`): "safe mode" writes `adjust_battery_target(100.0, False)` with no `inv_has_charge_enable_time` consult - on such a profile the target register carries the ESS minimum SoC, so 100% is a charge-to-100 order, not an idle value. Fire conditions: the 13 `reset_inverter: True` CONFIG items set `inverter_needs_reset` on any value change *including* None→value at startup (`userinterface.py`), and the two `reset_inverter_force` items (mode select, `set_read_only`) additionally set the force name on a user event - the forced paths run the reset even in Read-Only, where no further writes happen afterwards (the head of `reset_inverter()` gates on it, and `execute_plan()` skips the loop). Normally the 100 is corrected later the same `execute_plan()` cycle (`adjust_battery_target_multi(inverter, 0, ...)` for `has_charge_enable_time: False` inverters outside a window), so it stands only when nothing competes: entering Read-Only, or Predbat stopping right after the reset. Other 100% writes: the calibration branch (`execute.py`) and `self_test()` (only under the `INVERTER_TEST` dev constant). Fixture trap: the identically named `reset_inverter(my_predbat)` in `tests/test_infra.py` resets test state and is unrelated - grep-based test-coverage checks get fooled, and no test module covers the real write path. Docs trap: `docs/inverter-setup.md` used to link `templates/victron.yaml`, which did not exist (404, confirmed against the raw URL; the Victron route existed in prose and GH#2846's discussion only) - **fixed by PR #5332 (merged 2026-10-10, post-v9.3.7)**, which adds the template and rewrites the section; on a pre-#5332 checkout a "user following the docs" Victron report is following the issue discussion. | `inverter` |
| `Hold charging target X%-Y%` while the house still imports at a peak price, or a peak-price charge window in the plan | Three code paths, all verified by reading on main (GH#2716; the family covers #1690 and #5083, and the maintainer marked the original #2716 case fixed in Nov 2025): (1) *plan side* — the high-price gate (`plan.py`, `price_key > best_price_charge_level`) guards only windows whose limit is still 0, so a window already carrying a limit from the levelling pass is exempt and `discard_unused_charge_slots()` keeps any `limit > 0` slot with no price re-check — a peak-price charge window in the plan is not by itself gate evidence, run the config-before-code checks first; (2) *"Hold charging" can be an armed-window fallback, not a hold* — the window-disabling hold needs `set_reserve_hold` + `set_reserve_enable` + `reserve_max >= target` or `inv_has_timed_pause`, and without either the fallback re-arms the charge window while the status still reads "Hold charging"; (3) *the pre-window freeze write* — a freeze-flavoured window inside `set_soc_minutes` before charging starts logs `not yet in charge freeze, holding target SoC at 100%` and deliberately writes a 100% target, so a 100% slot visible in the inverter portal during a displayed hold is that write, not a rogue one. Forensic decoder: the status suffix in `Hold charging target X%-Y%` is the **SoC–target pair**, not window start–end; and while charging the executor writes `max(charge_limit_best[0], reserve)` as the target, so SoC rising past the target means the writes never landed or the inverter ignores mid-window limit reductions (unresolved — needs the brand). | `inverter`, `plan` |
| Plan table shows `HoldChrg -> N%` (and the status text "charging to N%") while the slot is actually charging to a higher limit | The State cell and the status text both classify from `charge_window_best[n]["target"]` — `calc_percent_limit(target, ...)` in `publish_html_plan()` (output.py), `HoldChrg` when it lands at/below the window's minimum predicted SoC — but `discard_unused_charge_slots()` only stamps `target = limit` when predicted SoC sits at/above the limit at both window start and end, and **otherwise `target` = predicted end-of-window SoC**: the rate-limited "charge can't complete" case. So a charge that cannot complete renders as `HoldChrg -> <predicted SoC>` and `get_charge_export_text()` says "charging to N%", while a genuine hold (`target == limit`) still shows HoldChrg. Freeze classification already reads the limit (`is_freeze_charge(limit)`), so the two classifications can also disagree. A rising charge keeps span-min below end-SoC, so completing charges label correctly *by accident* — a green `Chrg` in a quick manual check proves nothing about the classification input. GH#4445 (split from #4440, whose 4-Aug log is the live evidence; #4446 sibling fixed and closed), verified on main 2026-10-07 by reading + a scratch unit test on the publish_html_plan path, unmerged. Fixture gap: `test_plan_why_reason.py` builds charge windows **without** a `"target"` key, so only the `window.get("target", ...)` fallback path is exercised — pin it with windows whose `target` diverges from `charge_limit_best`. Fix shape: classify from the limit (the same percent `calc_percent_limit(limit, ...)`, one token at each site) and keep the predicted end-SoC as the debug parenthesis; that makes Chrg/HoldChrg consistent with the freeze check. |
