Not a bug in the tie-break itself — `test_plan_tiebreak.py` asserts exactly that rule on purpose. **Every one of these gates is absolute in the rate sensor's own unit, so the tariff's scale multiplies how hard they bind (GH#5261, verified by code read + parsing a plan-HTML attachment 2026-09-27):** `currency_symbols` is display/labelling only — the metric and every margin gate run in the raw unit of the rate sensor, so a tariff sensor scaled to c/kWh (the European templates that publish rates ×100, e.g. on Iberian dynamic tariffs) makes `metric_min_improvement_plan` (default 2.0, ≈ €0.02 there) sit against slot prices of 0.02-0.05 — the #4736 mechanism amplified ~20x, and `should_replace_plan()` never adopts a ~0.1-unit-better plan; the within-run `metric_min_improvement` defaults to 0.0, so retention is the binding gate. Start any cent-scale-or-negative-price "why didn't the plan X" report by computing the stake in the sensor's unit before comparing against a gate. A plan-HTML export attachment is parseable without a debug yaml: rows carry `data-minute`/`data-rate` on the import/export cells, merged charge windows appear as `rowspan` "Chrg" cells, and `rate_range_text()` renders the tooltip of a merged cell as a `{min}-{max}` range — a `(-0.05-0.05p/kWh)` tooltip is one window spanning differing rates, not one price. |
| Operation mode flip-flops between autonomous / self-consumption (or TBC / Self-Powered) within one control cycle and the export never engages — Tesla (and any service-template-controlled) inverter | **Fixed in PR #5114 (merged 2026-09-19)** — keep the mechanism for pre-#5114 logs. The `Charging/Discharging off via service` block (`execute.py`) had no `isExporting` guard: while an export is active `isCharging` is False, so it fired `adjust_charge_immediate(0)` → `charge_stop_service` 1-4s after `adjust_export_immediate()` had already run charge_stop + discharge_start, and on the shipped Tesla template both write the same operation-mode select, so the trailing write reverted the mode every cycle (GH#4165 and GH#4641, verified from both reporters' logs). #5114 skips the block while exporting — `adjust_export_immediate()` already stops charging as part of starting the export — and the `run_execute_test` status assertions now assert the charge-off never fires on `Exporting`/`Freeze exporting` statuses. Pre-fix log signature: `Calling service charge_stop_service` in a second pair *after* the discharge_start pair, within one 5-min cycle. On pre-fix versions `call_service_template`'s dedup would suppress the identical trailing call, but the shipped `templates/tesla_powerwall.yaml` sets `repeat: True` on every select write, which bypasses it — removing `repeat: True` from `charge_stop_service` is the user-side workaround. #4165 is the original; #4641 the dup. |
| Charge and hold writes alternate every 5 minutes (GivTCP/GE "Setting Charge Schedule to enable/disable" + pause-mode toggles swapping nightly) while the plan target is rock-stable | The reported SoC is flipping by exactly 1% between cycles across a mid-quantisation-band boundary, not plan churn — in the verified case the target held at 71% for hours (a fractional limit, 6.64/9.39 kWh = 70.7%, printed "71%") while GE's SoC read 71↔70. `execute.py`'s hold branch gates on `soc_percent >= inv_target_soc_percent` with no tolerance; the intended 1% hysteresis (`soc_percent >= inv_target_soc_percent - 1.0`, comment "or within 1% below it") is nested *inside* that gate, so it is always true when reached and its else (re-enable the charge window) is dead — any SoC even 0.1% below target falls to "Charging" and re-enables the window, and next cycle at target it holds again (GH#5083, structure verified on main). Quick confirmation from their log: grep `Charge window will be` and check the target stays constant while current SoC alternates ±1% — read the raw lines, because the strings "current SoC X%," and "target Y%" shift field positions and an awk one-liner can make the target *look* like it flips. The gate/dead-hysteresis structure predates July's #4164 and the reporter's v8.52→v8.53.3 onset window touched neither the hold path nor the plan gate — late-Aug onset was situational (a target landing dead-heat on the quantisation band), not a regression. Fix direction: widen the gate to `target - 1.0`. Related, not duplicates: #4232 (EMS charge-target spam), #3693 (bulk GivTCP writes), and `soc-kw quantised by percent sensor` (same class, different sensor path). |
| An inverter disappears from the plan after upgrading to v9.x (mixed fleet) | Start at the vendor component's `automatic_config()` and its `set_arg_auto(..., overwrite=True)` writes, not `execute.py`'s inverter loop. GH#5029: since the GivTCP REST refactor (#4864, v9.0.0+) GivTCP's auto-config wrote `inverter_type = ["GE" x discovered]` and `num_inverters = len(discovered)` (`givtcp.py`), so in a mixed fleet (GE on GivTCP REST + another brand configured manually) the manual `num_inverters` was shrunk and the non-GE inverter silently dropped out — the component-vs-manual instance of #4824. **Fixed in PR #5037 (v9.0.2)**: GivTCP's auto-config now only ever *raises* `num_inverters` (`_configured_num_inverters()` takes the largest value already configured) and `_keep_configured_tail()` preserved the configured list entries for any inverter beyond the discovered ones. PR #5216 (GH#5209) replaced `_keep_configured_tail()` with `_per_endpoint_values()`, which pins Predbat inverter n to REST endpoint n: the tail past the highest discovered endpoint is still kept as configured, but a gap slot below it (an endpoint that has not answered) keeps its configured entry or, with nothing configured, is bound to that endpoint's own not-yet-published entities, and `num_inverters` covers at least `max(discovered) + 1`. Keep the mechanism for pre-v9.0.2 logs: grep the reporter's full startup log for `auto-discovery wins` to confirm without needing their apps.yaml. `num_inverters`/`inverter_type` are **not** in `GIVTCP_AUTO_CONFIG_USER_WINS_KEYS` (that tuple is only the energy-today history keys, #4959) — don't cite that list as a guard. Fallback workaround at any version: `givtcp_automatic: False` (entities still published, auto-config skipped). Maintainer's preferred direction for the wider class: a per-component inverter offset, same spirit as the myenergi per-device-kind flag preference. |
| Inverter service calls silently stopped after an upgrade | Each log grep discriminates one hypothesis: `Calling service` present but the script never runs → HA-layer rejection, look for `was not accepted` / `Service call ... failed` (ha.py); `Skipped service ... as it was previously called` repeatedly → identical-payload dedup, the plan/payload is static, look at why target/power never change; **neither line at all** → the executor branch is never reached: `Exception raised` (run_time_loop logs the traceback) or `Failed to fetch inverter data, not able to execute the plan` — note plan sensors can still look live while execute is dead, so "the plan updates" does not prove `execute_plan()` runs. Also check `predbat.status` *during* a planned window: `Charging` = the executor is reaching the service calls; stuck `Demand` = the charge branch never entered. `last_service_hash` is in-memory only (`predbat.py`, `self.last_service_hash = {}`) — cleared by every restart and by `reset_inverter()` — so any "dedup state survived the migration" theory is dead on arrival. Only two users have hit a variant of this class (discussion #3373, GH#5021). **Second shape now confirmed (GH#5627, verified on main 2026-10-19): a multi-inverter Service-API fleet shares one dedup hash slot per domain** — `call_service_template()` keys its slot by `domain` only and lives on the shared Predbat object, so two inverters issuing different services on the same domain in one cycle stomp each other's slot every cycle and both are re-called every 5 minutes for as long as they diverge. Discriminator vs the identical-payload dedup above: here the log shows *alternating* `Calling service` lines of two different services on the same domain, with the per-domain `Skipped service` lines for the quiet domain still present alongside — and a skip "reported for inverter 1" can be decided by *inverter 0's* identical call landing in the shared slot milliseconds earlier, not by that inverter's own history. Fix direction (maintainer call): key the slot per inverter + domain — `test_inverter.py`'s dedup tests pin the current single-slot key space and are single-inverter, so divergence is untested. |
| `cost_yesterday` / the History view shows today's rates for yesterday, or `savings_yesterday` looks wrong on a dynamic tariff | `rate_replicate()` back-fills missing negative minutes with `minute % (24*60)` — today's same-time-of-day rate — and `calculate_yesterday()` then shifts the result back 24 h through `history_to_future_rates()`, so the History plan table (`cost_yesterday`) and the savings baseline are both simulated on today's rates (GH#5194, dup of #3267; verified on main by reading plus the reporter's dump, where `predbat.rates` matched hourly across 21→22 Sep). Exact on a fixed tariff; wrong every day on hourly-varying ones — nordpool and Energi Data Service expose no `raw_yesterday` attribute, so no real previous-day data reaches Predbat on the sensor-attribute path. The live plan is unaffected (it uses forward rates). The Octopus plugin's `_previous_day_rates` event is the in-tree pattern for a real previous-day source; #3267's fix direction is HA history of the rate sensor — note the collaborator's point that saving-session/Axle/IOG/override stamps make the live sensor a poor historical record even for today. |
| `Warn: Found N periods of zero load with power data, filling using power integration` repeating every 5-minute cycle | `fill_load_from_power()` (`fetch.py`), on by default (`load_power_fill_enable`, default True) whenever both `load_today` and `load_power` are configured. The scan re-runs on freshly-fetched history every cycle and nothing marks a flagged stretch handled, so one flat stretch stays Warn-ed for up to `max_days_previous` ≈ 8 days (GH#3623 is the open repeat-complaint issue). Two benign triggers, replay-verified in GH#5204: counter quantisation plateaus (at 200 W one 0.1 kWh step is 30 min, so ~34-40 min flat runs get flagged at non-zero base values) and the pre-helper zero region (minutes before a newly created counter existed are padded 0.0 while power has real data, giving multi-hour runs until they age out — normal for brand-new helpers). Benign and self-healing; silence with `load_power_fill_enable: false`. The detail line `Zero load period starting at <ts> ... for M minutes with base value V` names the window. Replay traps: `minute_data` history is oldest-first (feeding it newest-first silently produces an all-zero series); with `clean_increment` the output is a relative series re-anchored to 0 at the oldest point (`clean_incrementing_reverse()`), so "base value" is not the counter's reading; and with `backwards=True, smoothing=True` and no `to_key` the in-item interpolation loops never run — the series is built by the gap-fill tail/middle fills, so don't expect the smoothing branch to write anything when replaying. |
| Load history wrong or short after changing the load_today sensor (new inverter); load_today running away by kWh per hour on v9.4.1 (#5652); or `Filled N gaps in load_today totalling XkWh from predbat.load_energy_h0 history` (v9.4.1) / `Filled load_today before its history starts N minutes ago with XkWh ...` in the log | `fill_load_from_history()` (`fetch.py`, called from `fetch_load_today()`, on by default via `load_history_fill_enable`). Before it, a new load_today sensor's days before its first sample were padded flat by `minute_data()`, and since `minute_data_load()` reports the age of the youngest entity, `get_filtered_load_minute()` clamped every `days_previous` entry to `min(days, load_minutes_age)` and silently reused the oldest real day - not the average gap filler. The fill splices in increments from `predbat.load_energy_h0`'s HA history (it mirrors `load_minutes_now`: unscaled, not car/iBoost filtered, since midnight, summed across inverters; the docs said it was scaled by `load_scaling` but the code never did) over the flat run back from the oldest minute (the days before the sensor's first record), only when that run is at least a day long, then extends `load_minutes_age`. It runs before `fill_load_from_power()` and the average filler, and adds nothing where load_energy_h0 was flat too, so a dead sensor Predbat itself saw still falls through to them. Its `source` attribute (`load_today` / `ge_cloud` / `load_forecast`) lets forecast-only records be skipped. `predbat.load_energy_actual` is not a usable source: its state is actual-so-far plus the predicted rest of today, with car energy removed. Any trailing flat of a day or more (history shorter than `max_days_previous`) makes it fetch load_energy_h0 history every cycle. **#5652 (v9.4.1 regression):** v9.4.1 filled *any* flat stretch of `load_filter_threshold` or longer, mid-history included. load_energy_h0 is published from the filled `load_minutes_now`, so each cycle read back the last cycle's fill as measured history and today's load climbed every 5 minutes: two Solis Cloud SaaS instances reached 139 / 116 kWh in a day against ~10 / ~4 kWh real. Solis' whole-kWh counter, refreshed hourly, keeps a flat open almost permanently. The seed is not only the fill: even when h0 is published raw, h0 records lag the sensor by up to a cycle and smoothing spreads each counter step back into the flat before it (simulated: 7.2 kWh against a real 4.0), so load_energy_h0 recorded from the *same* sensor is never safe to fill from. Tagging records with the source sensor was rejected because SaaS history keeps only the state, not attributes; the `source` attribute (and so the `load_forecast` skip) only works on HA and Predbat's own DB. The fix fills only before load_today's first record. The day minimum is there because history that only lists changes (Predbat's `db_engine` query has no window-start record) can open hours into the window on a quiet night. `test_coarse_counter_does_not_run_away` replays a day of cycles with state-only history; it reaches 163 kWh on v9.4.1's code, and the fix is merged (PR #5656, 2026-10-20). Also for pre-fix (v9.4.1) logs: the `Filled N gaps in load_today totalling XkWh` total sums **every** in-window gap minute across `max_days_previous` days plus a boundary artefact near the array's padded end, so it is much larger than today's contribution — do not read it as "today's inflation" (a multi-cycle simulation on synthetic data puts the first-day double-count at material-but-settling, +3.6–7.3 kWh day 1, not the unbounded runaways the two reporters saw — suspected unverified amplifiers there: real recording patterns, non-monotone counters, dp3 wiggle). load_energy_h0 history published by v9.4.1 stays polluted for `max_days_previous` days, but is now only read for days before a sensor's first record. Trap for any sensor Predbat both publishes and reads back: `minute_data(interpolate=True)` extrapolates past a newest record under 15 minutes old, which a sensor republished every cycle always is, so the extrapolation lands in the next published value and compounds - load_energy_h0 climbed at the last rate seen, 0.083 kWh per cycle at 1 kW, while load_today was silent (PR #5570 review). Read such sensors with `interpolate=False`, and do not let smoothing join records across an unmeasured stretch. |
| Two charge windows meeting at exactly 00:00 inside one continuous cheap import period (optionally with an export window squeezed between) | `calc_dawn()`'s one-way light latch resets at each calendar-day boundary (deliberate — a polar-night day must stay all-dark and a polar-day day all-light; the docstring on `calc_dawn` records why), so an evening after that morning's real dawn is latched "light" while the following pre-dawn buckets classify "dark" even though both are physically dark with zero PV: `pv_light_dark` steps 1→0 at every midnight, and `find_charge_window()` treats any in-window light/dark transition as a boundary (GH#5205, replay-verified against a v9.1.0 dump). Fires only with `combine_charge_slots: true` — with it off, windows are already broken every `charge_slot_split` minutes before a dawn boundary can be reached (`calc_pv_light_dark()` docstring). Once split, a charge-export-charge cycle has to clear the usual export economics gate, so the cycle is not itself a bug — the artificial split is. Fixed on main by PR #5709 (2026-10-23): `find_charge_window()` (`fetch.py`) splits a charge window only on a **dark → light** (dawn) step; the midnight light→dark reset no longer splits, so a cheap window spanning midnight stays whole when `combine_charge_slots: true`. |
| `apps.yaml has N errors` naming a `sensor.<custom-prefix>_<InverterType>_<id>_<arg>` entity that always reads a constant (Eco / on / 100) | Predbat's own dummy placeholder failing `validate_config()` because the `modify: True` exemption is hardcoded to the default prefix: `if sensor.startswith("sensor.predbat_")` (`predbat.py`) while `prefix` is a user-settable option, so any custom prefix (e.g. `predbatsolcast`) makes Predbat's own control placeholders fail validation (GH#5242, verified by reading on main and v9.1.0 — identical). A `sensor.{prefix}_{type}_{id}_*` entity with a constant state is Predbat's own placeholder (inverters without GE-style mode control get one for `inverter_mode` = "Eco", `inverter.py`), not the integration's entity; idle-time dummies are not in `APPS_SCHEMA` so never validated. Impact is cosmetic — `arg_errors` feeds only the `config_valid`/`config_warnings` sensors and the Apps-page counter; it does not block Active/control. The exemption dates to #2137, the dummy path to #1674; fix direction is exempting `sensor.{self.prefix}_` instead of the literal. |
| `apps.yaml has N errors` naming one of the `*_today` accumulators "is not a valid type float", repeating a few times a day at odd hours | Nothing wrong with apps.yaml, and nothing midnight-specific (GH#5421, verified against main `82bee43e`, probe-verified in `test_validate_config.py`): `validate_config()` (predbat.py:1532) reads **live entity states**, so an accumulator sensor unreadable at validation time (`unavailable`/`unknown`) comes back from `get_state_wrapper` as a **string** that passes the missing-entity `None` test and then fails the float check — a *missing* entity errors separately as `returned value 'None'`, which `transient_ok` can never loosen. `transient_ok` exists only for `car_charging_energy`/`car_charging_power` (`config.py`, added for GH#3667); the `load_today`/`import_today`/`export_today`/`pv_today` items carry none, so they falsely fail in rollover windows. Validation runs at startup plus every `update_pending` trigger — the 8-hour `CONFIG_REFRESH_PERIOD` staleness timer, websocket reconnects, UI changes, component publishes — so "errors several times a day" is that cadence landing inside a rollover window, and a reporter's recorder can show the sensor bad for **hours** (GH#5421's ran midnight→07:11), not a blip. Probe-verified fix direction: extend `transient_ok` to the four `*_today` args — missing-entity and non-numeric cases still error. Runtime is unaffected either way (`minute_data()` skips unavailable/unknown samples and fetch.py's own no-data warnings remain the guard for a genuinely dead sensor), and the retry ladder (`validate_config_retries`, GH#4379) covers startup races only — don't cite it as a rollover-window fix. Fixture trap: `test_validate_config.py` reports ~28-32 baseline `arg_errors` (regex-pointer givtcp entities reading `None`), so the total count is meaningless — assert per field. |
| "I can only override one plan slot at a time" / the override box closes after each set | Working as designed on the plan UI: each slot's override box sends **one** `POST /rate_override` per action and the JS reloads the page 1s after success (`web_helper.py` `handleRateOverride`/`handleLoadOverride`; the reload closing the box is deliberate, and the handler sets `update_pending`/`plan_valid` so the plan recomputes) — `html_rate_override` (`web.py`) writes exactly one `"Wed 23:00=0.15"`-style option per call into `manual_import_rates`/`manual_export_rates`/`manual_load_adjust`. Batch overrides already exist outside the plan UI (GH#5289): `rates_import_override`/`rates_export_override` in apps.yaml take a list of `{start, end, rate}` windows, and the manual API keeps each no-index command as a separate stackable window (`docs/energy-rates.md`, `docs/manual-api.md`; shape in `tests/test_basic_rates.py` rate4/rate5) — point a reporter at those before treating it as a feature request needing new plumbing. |
| Turning `debug_enable` on changes the plan (or a "the kernel wasn't active" claim contradicts its own scan count) | Deliberate engine switch, present since the kernel was introduced (#4169), not a v9.x regression: `kernel_supported()` (`prediction_kernel.py`) requires `not save and not pred.debug_enable and kernel_handle != 0`, so with the debug switch on **every** trial prediction takes the Python engine (the batch path applies the same check) - and with the kernel dispatching, the SoC min/max range of charge-window jobs is computed inline by the kernel, so Python `Prediction.scan_soc_range` is never reached. The tells: a debug-off run's 0 `scan_soc_range` count *requires* a non-zero `kernel_handle` (the Python fallback would call it for every min/max job in both configurations), and the committed per-architecture libraries load from the module's own directory with no installer - so "the kernel was not present unless the installer ran" is self-contradicting when their own debug-off scan count is 0 (GH#5408, reproduced at probe level on main 2026-10-05). The plan flip itself needs a near-tie: #4453's diagnosis is that the levels pass requests a coarse step (`enable_fast_mode_levels`, plan.py, default on) that the Python engine honours while the kernel always simulates at full 5-minute resolution, so the fast pass changes candidate inputs and can tip near-tied candidates (measured there: 0.0056p on a ~1605p plan) - identical plans debug-on/off are the expected result on a non-tie fixture, and the 2h auto-disable (`DEBUG_ENABLE_MAX_HOURS`, `const.py`) bounds how long the switch keeps the slow engine. Two loose ends live on main: `predbat.py`'s `_debug_enable_auto_scope()` docstring calls the Python path "a more accurate but far slower prediction path" while `kernel_supported()`'s docstring says kernel runs are both faster *and more accurate* than a coarse-step Python run - one of the two comments is wrong (the #4453 analysis supports the kernel docstring), and no user-facing doc says enabling debug re-runs planning on a different engine. |
| WhatIf (annual tool) run-details table or a run label shows 0 kWp for a panels-mode array | The model is right, two display readers are wrong: `_build_results()` stores the **raw** pre-validation config (`results["config"] = self.config["raw"]`, annual.py) while a panels-mode array (panels + panel_watts, no `kwp` key) gets its `kwp` derived only in `_validate_solar()` and never written back, so `_render_run_details()` (web_annual.py) and `build_label()` (annual_store.py) — which sum `array.get("kwp", 0)` over that raw config — render 0 kWp, while the engine's model and the compare table's `total_kwp` (built from the validated config) are correct (GH#4395, still live on v9.4.2; verified by test: 6.16 kWp modelled against the rendered "0 kWp across 14 panels in 2 arrays"). Fix shape when taken: the two display readers derive kWp when the array carries `panels` — and do **not** write the derived kwp into the stored raw config, `validate_config` rejects an array carrying both `kwp` and `panels`. Second display gap on the same surface: `web_annual.py`'s `_arg()` wrapper forwards no `required_unit`, so its per-inverter `soc_max`/`inverter_limit`/`export_limit` sums are unit-blind — a per-inverter sensor list reporting **kW** is summed raw and renders 1000x small (PR #5720 added per-entry `required_unit` forwarding to `resolve_arg`'s combine branch, merged 2026-10-24, but this wrapper passes no unit and so bypasses it). |
| Sigenergy: no MQTT data on any system and plan commands have no effect, while the log shows `SigenergyAPI: MQTT published to openapi/instruction/command` and REST reads work | Check the broker before the code: `python sigenergy.py --app-key K --app-secret S --system-id X --command-test --command-mode idle --command-duration 2` (`mqtt_command_probe()` / `command_probe_verdict()`). The `MQTT published` line proves nothing - aiomqtt discards the PUBACK reason code, MQTT 3.1.1 cannot signal a refused publish, and `_mqtt_listener_loop` ignores SUBACK results, so a broker that drops everything looks healthy in Predbat's own log. Two library-level facts behind that, verified from the installed aiomqtt/paho sources 2026-10-20: the production paths (`_publish_mqtt`, `_mqtt_listener_loop`) pass **no `protocol=`** to `aiomqtt.Client`, so runtime publishes ride aiomqtt's default **MQTT 3.1.1** and can never carry a reason code even against a V5 broker; and aiomqtt's `publish()` **does** wait for the PUBACK (a registered `asyncio.Event`, 10s default timeout on no ack) — "publish returned" means the PUBACK arrived, so the log line is only blind to the *reason*, not to the arrival. The probe is the only path that connects with `ProtocolVersion.V5`, and since the merged #5654 it warns when paho's `on_publish` cannot be hooked (aiomqtt internals changed) rather than silently losing capture. The probe connects over MQTT 5 and prints each SUBACK/PUBACK code. **Verified 2026-10-20 on a live VPP-mode system:** subscriptions to the documented `openapi/{change,period,alarm}/APP_KEY/SYSTEM_ID` topics were granted, but every publish to `openapi/subscription/*` and to `openapi/instruction/command` (topic confirmed against Sigenergy's API docs) got PUBACK `0x10 No matching subscribers`, no message arrived in 60s, and an `idle` command left the battery discharging 0.4kW to the load with SoC still falling (REST `energyFlow`), so it was not actioned; REST `/v1/history` returned `code 1001 rpc fail` at the same time. That combination is a Sigenergy-side backend outage, not a Predbat fault - confirmed when it recovered by itself the same morning (by 09:50 BST the `openapi/subscription/*` publishes were back to PUBACK `0x00`, with no change on Predbat's side). Use `idle`, not the default `selfConsumption`, when checking the battery reacted - with no PV, self-consumption looks identical to an ignored command. To check whether the topics have moved rather than gone quiet, `--mqtt-scan` subscribes to a ladder of wildcards (system- and app-key-scoped first, `openapi/#`, `#`, `$SYS/#` last) and prints each SUBACK code with its MQTT 5 reason string, any CONNACK properties (e.g. a server reference) and every topic that delivers, retained ones included; `--mqtt-topic` adds filters. Suspected only: `0x10` would also appear if their broker fed the backend through a rule engine rather than a subscription, so treat it as evidence alongside the battery check, not proof on its own. |
| Sigenergy: `API error code=1201 msg=Access restriction for /openapi/system` at startup, retried through the whole backoff | `/openapi/system` returns every system on the app key and Sigenergy throttles it globally (once per five minutes, per Sigenergy's docs), so Predbat instances sharing a key collide on it. `discover_systems()` now confirms a configured `system_id` from its own device list (`/openapi/system/{id}/devices`, throttled per system) and never reads the system list when a `system_id` is set - only with none configured, as the only way to enumerate. **Verified 2026-10-20:** a system the app key is not authorised for - a valid not-onboarded system and an invalid ID alike - gets **HTTP 403** on its device list, not a Sigenergy error code. So a 403 puts the system in `_not_onboarded` and `run()` calls `onboard_systems()` directly, whose per-item `codeList` says why (a live system registered to another VPP returned top-level `code 0` with `result: false, codeList: [1103]`); after a successful onboard it re-reads the device list, or returns False to retry. `_request` does not retry a 403 (it used to try five times, 6s apart) and records it in `_last_http_status`. Anything else - `1201`, a failed request, another API error, an empty list - leaves the system in `_unconfirmed_systems` and `run()` returns False to retry: it must never fall through to onboarding. `onboard_systems()` clears `_last_api_code` before its request, because `_request` only sets it on an API error and a stale code would be read as the outcome. Two traps behind this: the system-list body was logged in full (other customers' names and addresses), and `ratedEnergy` on the device list is kWh (9.04 per module, verified 2026-10-20 against a system-list `batteryCapacity` of 45.2) although an older response gave Ah (314), so `_get_battery_capacity_kwh()` treats only values above 100 as Ah - read as Ah, a 45.2kWh system came to 1.3kWh. |
| Plan-table times (or a manual override dropdown) are shifted by an hour on a clock-change day | The data plane and the labels live on different frames: rate/slot minute keys are true instant offsets from local midnight (`minute_data()` diffs tz-aware `last_updated` stamps, so the doubled hour prices correctly on the fall-back day — the data side needs nothing), while plan labels are wall arithmetic — `self.midnight_utc + timedelta(minutes=...)` across `output.py`'s label sites (plan tables, the manual time pickers in `userinterface.py`) — and `midnight_utc` keeps whatever offset the cycle ran with (see the "`midnight_utc` is local midnight" trap below). Direction depends on cycle timing: computed before the change, the second 02:00 labels 03:00 and everything after shifts +1h; computed after, the first 02:00 labels 01:00 — the same defect either way, so ask which the cycle did before reading anything into a variant (GH#5708, closed as a high-confidence dup of the open #1566, which carries the maintainer's one-line cause — search old DST-numbered issues first). The manual-override parser inherits the same frame: `get_override_time_from_string()` (utils.py) resolves a slot via `now_utc.replace(hour=...)` — on now's offset — so a slot crossing a changeover resolves on the wrong instant; PR #5729 (open at the 2026-10-27 flush) fixes it and renders the picker labels with `.astimezone(now_utc.tzinfo)`; whether the *dispatch-write* path shares the defect is unverified (both reporters saw no wrong inverter commands); `octopus.py`'s `_localise_wall_clock()` is the in-tree localise pattern from the #5655 day/night fix; #3688 / closed #3694 are the spring-forward sides. **Settling a changeover-day report from the dump alone (GH#5734, closed as a dup of #1566; both checks verified against the reporter's attachment, 2026-10-24):** the debug yaml's minute-keyed `rate_import` block stores one entry per line with the first key at −1440, so minute *m* sits *first-rate_import-line + m + 1440* — find the free-session/override zero band or the cheap band and convert its minute keys to true instants (they are elapsed minutes from local midnight with the offset carried: on 24 Oct BST, midnight = 23:00Z); #5734's zero band [2220, 2340) decoded to 12:00Z–14:00Z = real wall 12:00–14:00, i.e. **the data plane was already correct and only labels were shifted** — a verdict with no code run. And the free-session log line is label-shifted too: `Setting Octopus free session in range ...` (`octopus.py`) formats through `time_abs_str()` (`predbat.py`), the same fixed-offset wall arithmetic as the plan card and override dropdown, so a +1h range in that line on changeover day is *not* evidence the session moved; `load_free_slot()` does no timezone arithmetic of its own (str2time → minutes_to_time, instant-true). Expected companion shape on fall-back day: a wall-time cheap block spanning the changeover covers 6 real hours but its label stretches to 7 wall hours. Render-offset directions verified for GH#5749 (code-read + a standalone run of the real `update_time()`/`minutes_to_time()` render chain, 2026-10-25): within one DST regime the frames agree exactly; across a change, an instant *after* the change rendered with the pre-change midnight's fixed offset labels one hour **late** (autumn) / **early** (spring), and an instant *before* the change re-rendered after it labels one hour early (a slot start `23:30+01:00` re-rendered from a `+00:00` midnight prints `22:30`); rendering through `(midnight_utc + timedelta(minutes=m)).astimezone(self.local_tz)` before `strftime` handles all three cases and a fresh decode after the change is already correct — so a wrong window that *persists* past the change is being re-rendered (retained plan / throttled recompute / saved-slot expansion), not re-derived; that persistence route is suspected, not verified (needs the reporter's change-day dump; `io_adjusted` resets each cycle, so the expansion half lives component-side). No test covers the change-night write: the only DST tests are the `test_energydataservice_dst_*` rates-layer ones (from #5655), so `execute_plan`-at-change-eve is the missing golden check. | |
| `cost_yesterday` / `savings_yesterday_*` show the wrong day's figures after a clock change (or the History view's whole yesterday timeline shifts) | A history-**lookup** frame error, not the rates (`rate_replicate()`'s yesterday-fill row above is the dynamic-tariff variant of the same symptom): `minute_data()` keys its dict by real-elapsed minutes from `last_updated` (utils.py), but on a change day the wall-clock `minutes_now` diverges from real elapsed by exactly ±60 for the rest of that day (GH#5760, reproduced end-to-end on main ef1432be with Predbat's own `minute_data()` plus a two-day `cost_today` history carrying the reporter's values): `calculate_yesterday()` reads yesterday's final values at `minutes_back = minutes_now + 1` (output.py), which on the autumn morning lands at 00:59 of the *new* day — reading the standing charge (47.47) instead of the previous day's 23:59 total — and keeps reading the wrong value until the next midnight; after the spring change it reads yesterday's 22:59, dropping the final hour. The whole `minutes_back` lookup family shifts with it: `cost_yesterday_per_kwh`, `carbon_yesterday`, the `cost_yesterday_array`/`battery_soc_yesterday_array` behind the History view (whole yesterday timeline shifts), `battery_soc_yesterday`, `cost_yesterday_car`, and `yesterday_adjustment_pct` in the in-day load adjustment (bounded in practice — clamped 0.5–2.0 and blended toward today's own factor — skews rather than breaks). The `savings_total_*` totals themselves stay correct: the day-rollover rebuild runs on the first cycle after local midnight, still inside the pre-change regime, so the totals get correct figures once per change day. **Do not "fix" `minutes_now`** — its docstring (utils.py) documents wall-clock-across-DST by design and it is a slot index everywhere; the fix belongs at the lookup/render sites or a real-elapsed helper (localize the *wall* midnight of `now_utc.date()`, then subtract), while #5749's `astimezone` render fix (row above) is the write-path counterpart — the two symptoms need the two sites. Europe/London's change-day 00:00 is never ambiguous, so that localize form is sound there; zones that change at midnight (ambiguous/nonexistent 00:00) need `is_dst` care, untested. Test gap: `test_calculate_yesterday` passes with normal-day indexes — nothing covers a change day; the probe shape is `update_time()`'s construction plus the `minutes_now + 1` lookup across autumn/spring/sanity days. | |
| A live export (or charge) window stops part-way through a slot and its start moves to the next slot boundary, with `Dynamic load last period 9.60kW, status high` / `Dynamic load status changed from low to high` in the cycle just before, while the house load is ordinary | A coarse energy counter faking a dynamic load "high". `load_last_period` is the 5-minute difference of `load_today`, set in `fetch_load_today()` before `fill_load_from_power()` smooths the series, so a counter that moves in whole kWh reads as 0 or ~12 kW. Solis' `homeLoadTotalEnergy` does, on every site measured (44, 28 Oct 2026). `dynamic_load_classify()` calls each step "high", and `dynamic_load()` raises the load for the rest of the slot and re-plans. Verified on a Solis site on 27 Oct 2026: 9.60 kW and 7.20 kW against a live `load_power` under 0.8 kW, each a few minutes after a counter step, each moving a live export window's start. PR #5851 makes a high reading need the live `load_power` to confirm it. On an older log, compare the live load in the same cycle before trusting a dynamic load "high". |

## Traps when investigating

- **Stale kernel binary.** The `prediction_kernel_lib_*.so` binaries are committed and CI has a job for them. The warning above means the checkout falls back to the Python engine, which is fine for triage. Never rebuild or commit binaries while triaging. Two engine-active tells when triaging a plan-difference question: the libraries load from the module's own directory with no installer step (so "the kernel was not installed" is usually wrong in a checkout), and **a 0 `scan_soc_range` (`prediction.py`) call count over a full plan means the C++ kernel served it** - the Python fallback calls it for every min/max job, so a non-zero count proves the Python engine ran (GH#5408; a report claiming "the kernel was not active" whose debug-off scan count is 0 is self-contradicting).
- **A new kernel context array must be copied into `ContextStore`.** `pk_context_create` (`prediction_kernel.cpp`) copies every per-step array into vectors it owns, because the ctypes buffers built by `create_kernel_context` are freed once it returns. Adding a field to `PkContext` and the ctypes mirror without the matching `ContextStore` vector, `assign` and `.data()` lines leaves a dangling pointer, and it does not crash: the first `kernel_parity` run gave a plausible but wrong `metric_keep` (one half hour of a credit missing), and a rerun gave values near 1e191 across unrelated cases. Verified while adding `io_credit` (bird-in-hand credit, 2026-10-18): adding the three lines fixed both. Delete the local `prediction_kernel_lib.so` before re-running parity after a `.cpp` edit, as an existing loadable build is not rebuilt.
- **A planner test that flips from the Python engine to the kernel can pick a different plan with no parity bug.** Moving `iog_bird_in_hand` to the kernel (2026-10-20, 32s to 1.5s) changed the off-peak charge limits in every scenario (Python 10.0, kernel 4.0-6.25) while both plans scored identically on both engines. A probe that wrapped `prediction.run_prediction_kernel` and `prediction_batch.run_prediction_kernel_batch` and re-ran each job on the Python engine (`kernel_handle = 0`, `cache=False`) found all 2149 five-minute jobs matching exactly and every one of the 90 mismatches a `step=30` job - the levels pass's fast mode, which the Python engine honours and the kernel ignores (it always simulates at 5 minutes). With `fast=False` forced into `optimise_charge_limit_price_threads`, the two engines chose identical plans in all six scenarios, matching the kernel's. Dead end: setting `enable_fast_mode_levels: False` is *not* enough to rule fast mode out, because the region passes in `optimise_levels_pass` (`plan.py`) set `fast_mode = not (region_size == min_region_size)` themselves and ignore that setting.
- **`minutes_now = 0` hides time-of-day bugs.** The load-forecast tests set `minutes_now = 0` in their shared
  setup, which made GH#4732's holiday off-by-one invisible for years: the faulty `tod <= minutes_now` was then
  true only for the midnight slot. Anything indexing history as `(minutes_now - tod) + d * 1440` needs a test at
  a non-zero `minutes_now`, and wall-clock-aligned fixture days (`test_holiday_mode.py`) rather than the
  minutes-ago bucketing in `test_load_forecast_history.py`, which only lines up when `minutes_now` is 0.
- **Golden plans move when load scaling changes.** Any change to `step_data_history`'s `scale_today` handling
  shifts both `coverage/cases/*.expected.json` (regenerate by copying the `*.actual.json` the run writes) and
  `coverage/cases/random_results.json` (regenerate with `./run_random`, then copy `random_results.json` over it).
  `debug_cases` stops at the first failing case, so a second case can start failing only after you fix the first.
  Before regenerating, confirm the shift is the intended one by temporarily restoring the old behaviour and
  checking the golden files pass again.
- **Build test dates from Predbat's clock, not the machine's.** Predbat runs on the timezone set in apps.yaml (Europe/London in the harness), not the host's, so a fixture built with `datetime.now()` is on a different date from the code under test for part of every day. `test_saving_session` and `test_alert_feed` failed exactly that way under `TZ=America/Los_Angeles` at 21:30 local - and the clearest case is instructive: `load_free_slot()` measures a slot against `midnight_utc`, so a window built for the machine's "today" starts before Predbat's day began and is dropped entirely. Anchor fixture dates to `my_predbat.midnight_utc` (`96e1073c`, PR #4877). Hardcoded future dates in test data have the same shelf-life problem and were pushed out to 2099 (`d850365b`). Newest instance: PR #4971's `multi_car_iog` tests force `now_utc` to 12:00 on the *machine's* UTC date while `midnight_utc` stays on the real clock, so between 23:00 UTC and midnight the forced slot falls behind local midnight and is dropped - observed as `multi_car_iog_load_slots_regression` and `multi_car_iog_model_limit_fetch_4967` both failing with "car 0 should have IOG slots, got none" at 00:06 local, on a clean docs-only tree (mechanism reasoned from the code, not reproduced at a passing hour) - **fixed in PR #4998** by pinning `midnight_utc` in those tests, keep the mechanism for the next test that pins only one of the two clocks. Newest instance (2026-09-20 00:12 BST): `test_teslemetry_local_weekday_follows_the_base_clock` asserts its no-base fallback against the *host's* `datetime.now().weekday()` while the fallback itself is `datetime.now(timezone.utc)` — the two clocks disagree in the hour after local midnight (UTC is still the previous day), so the module crashes in that window; observed on a clean docs-only tree, and seen again 2026-09-22 00:09 BST aborting the quick suite mid-run. Counterweight for a different clock: storage-cache expiry is compared against the **real** clock (see the Storage row in the per-integration table), so anything producing or asserting an expiry must use the real one — the discriminator is which clock the code under test compares against. **Newest instance (2026-10-01 00:15 BST, found while flushing this queue):** `solax` fails the quick suite 00:00-01:00 local in summer — `test_query_plant_statistics_daily_main` asserts the queried month as `datetime.now()` (local) while the component derives it with `datetime.now(timezone.utc)` (`query_plant_statistics_daily()`, `solax.py`), and the two disagree while UTC is still on the previous day; nothing aborts, the module just reports `SolaX API tests FAILED:` with an **empty detail** after a long run of green subtests (the silently-set `failed` flag trips an `assert not failed` gate without a message), so grep the module output for the `**** ERROR:` lines — the month-prefix mismatch line is the only pointer.
- **`midnight_utc` is local midnight, and `now_utc` is local time.** Both names are wrong in the same direction and it has misled at least one review into hunting for a UTC-vs-local frame mismatch that does not exist. `update_time()` sets `now_utc_real = datetime.now(self.local_tz)` (`predbat.py:654`) - already local, not UTC - and `midnight_utc` is that value with the time zeroed (`predbat.py:655`), i.e. the local midnight of Predbat's configured timezone as a timezone-aware datetime. Anything comparing a slot against `midnight_utc` is therefore working in local wall-clock, which is usually what you want and never what the name says. Before concluding a rate or slot bug is a timezone frame error, check which of the two frames the code is actually in. Corollary for reading logs: `time_abs_str()` renders against `self.midnight`, so a line quoting a past/negative minute key prints in the local frame and will not line up with the UTC timestamps an integration's sensor publishes (GH#5036) — recompute the minutes from the sensor's own timestamps instead of keying evidence off the printed date. Component-side twin, verified separately (GH#5572, `component_base.py`, main 697c44ca): `ComponentBase.midnight_utc` deliberately derives from `base.now_utc` with the time zeroed — **not** from `base.midnight_utc`, which `calculate_yesterday()` rewinds and components read off-thread (its docstring records both reasons) — so it is likewise the **local** midnight as an aware datetime, and formatting it straight with `strftime("...Z")` puts a local wall-clock behind a `Z` suffix, an hour out all summer; any component building an API query window (`period_from`, GraphQL `startAt`) from it must `.astimezone(timezone.utc)` first, and a winter-dated (GMT) test cannot catch the mistake — pin a BST date. Contrast: `kraken.py`'s own GraphQL fallbacks build their window from `datetime.now(timezone.utc)` — genuine UTC midnight, a different instant from the component property during BST (harmless there given the day of margin either side, but the two windows don't line up exactly). Same class, one over: `ComponentBase.now_utc_exact` is local time too — `datetime.now(self.local_tz)` (`component_base.py`; the name reads like a UTC clock, the docstring already says local) — and the GH#5693 issue-author's UTC assumption understated their own bug's impact; `db_manager.set_state_db()` treats it as its "now" and then applies its own broken UTC conversion on top (see the DB row).
- **Test ordering.** Since PR #5102 (merged 2026-09-15) every registry test builds its own `PredBat` (GH#5079), so the shared-fixture pollution class is fixed at the source — a test that fails in a full run but passes alone is now likelier to be one of the remaining hand-rolled restores (test_inverter.py, test_agent_tools.py; see the GH#5079 section) or a genuinely order-dependent case, and running only the targeted test still isolates. `run_debug_cases` builds a fresh instance per case for its own reason: each case replays a full dump. The wall-clock half is closed too: `create_predbat()` now pins `minutes_now`/`now_utc` from `FIXTURE_MINUTES_NOW` (`test_infra.py`), so no module inherits either the wall clock or another module's residue into those two fields — the fixture-level pin the GH#5026 note anticipated has landed and closes that class (keep the mechanism for pre-#5102 logs: a suite run used to sit at `reset_inverter`'s noon residue while a standalone run inherited the wall clock, and a time-of-day-dependent test could pass one way and fail the other — `prediction_batch` failed exactly that way, the ten-trial SoC-cost precondition collapsing onto one value at seed 5's window/rate alignment; equivalence-style subtests are `minutes_now`-insensitive, only precondition checks on seeded-scenario economics are sensitive). The per-module pins that preceded the fixture pin: PR #5028 (merged, `9290055d`) pinned the clock per-module - the third per-module pin of this class, after PR #5013 pinned dates and #4971's tests pinned `midnight_utc` - with `minutes_now` added to `SCENARIO_STATE_ATTRS`. Two caveats outlive the fixture pin: a pin-guard test is **vacuous in suite configuration when the pin value equals the residue value** (suite ambient was already 720), so a guard test must capture the pre-pin entry value rather than compare against the pin constant; and `update_time()` still takes `midnight_utc`/`now_utc_real` from the host clock, so the date-frame half of this trap is unchanged.
- **`./run_all --test <name>` resolves names through `TEST_REGISTRY` (`unit_test.py`), not module or file names - a green run proves nothing when the probe was never executed.** `--test dynamic_load_car` maps to `tests/test_dynamic_load.py::test_dynamic_load_car_slot_cancellation` (which runs only that module's Tests 1/2/5), while `tests/test_dynamic_load_car.py` is registered under the name `dynamic_load_car_not_charging` - so probing the car-not-charging tests under the obviously-correct-looking name is a green run with zero new assertions executed (GH#5407 triage). Check `TEST_REGISTRY` before building a probe; and inside `test_dynamic_load_car.py` the `_run_edges`/`_run_rates`/`_run_poll`/... group runs only when the dynamic-load-car args are enabled in the fixture, so a probe wired into that block silently never runs under the default fixture either - wire it into the module entry function after `_run()`.
- **A textually clean auto-merge can still be a semantic conflict.** Merging `origin/main` into an old PR branch reported no conflicts while main's GivTCP refactor (#4864) had deleted `Inverter.rest_data`, which the PR both tested on and passed to the constructor - the merged tree crashed the first `Inverter()` construction in two test modules, only one of them the PR's own (PR #4645). For PR-cleanup work the full `run_pre_commit` suite is the detector, not the PR's own module. Corollary: `rest_data` is gone since #4864 and `inverter_source_active()` (`inverter.py`) is the replacement question for "is this inverter component/REST-backed" - anything written against `self.rest_data` predates the refactor. And when a test plants a components stub, construction-time calls run against whatever stub the *previous* test module left behind, so a new components-consulting call in `Inverter.__init__` needs a `getattr` guard on the registry surface, not a fake patched into one module. Correction to what this bullet itself used to imply: `inverter_source_active()` answers a **fleet-wide** question, not a per-inverter one — it takes no index and consults no per-inverter state, so one component-backed inverter at index 0 makes it true for a hand-configured, script-driven inverter at index 1 (its docstring says the scope is deliberate). There is no per-index source API; the usable proxy was the shape of the per-inverter args list itself, because auto-config wrote slots `0..n_discovered-1` and `_keep_configured_tail()` left the rest as the user wrote them — a list that names some inverters but not this one meant the source had said what it covers and this index was outside it (PR #4645, still open, gates on that proxy). **The proxy weakened with PR #5216 (GH#5209):** GivTCP now fills slot n from REST endpoint n via `_per_endpoint_values()`, so a gap slot below the highest discovered endpoint is filled even when nothing is configured for it (with that endpoint's own entities), and a full-length list no longer implies every index is component-covered. Only indices past `max(discovered)` still follow the old rule; a per-index gate has to compare against the component's entity-id prefix (`<prefix>_givtcp_<n>_`) rather than the list length. Related: `create_missing_arg()` pads short per-inverter lists with `None`, not the default, precisely so an unfilled slot is not read back as an entity id — a hand-rolled `while len(...) <= id: append(default)` loop next to it is both dead and wrong about the padding value.
- **Check a PR's base staleness before reviewing it.** A long-open PR's stored base commit can be well behind main, and a conflicting PR's headline fix may already be on main: PR #4756's `plan_iboost_smart()` fix duplicated the merged GH#4817 window-average fix, and resolving its `test_iboost.py` conflict in the PR's favour would have silently deleted main's `run_iboost_smart_average_test` — resurrecting the bug. Check `git merge-base --is-ancestor <base> main` and diff any test-file changes against main's current tests, not the PR base, before analysing the "fix".
- **A stacked PR's squash can sit on the review target's head branch (PR #4186 round, 2026-10-28).** GitHub merges a stacked PR into its base *branch*, not main, so the squash lands on the target PR's head branch: #5811 merged into #4186's head branch (`feat/plan-execute-split`), the tip commit's message carried the `(#5811)` squash suffix while the PR was #4186, and #4186's GitHub diff became merge-base..head spanning both PRs — matching `git rev-parse HEAD == headRefOid` proves nothing. Before reviewing PR N, check the head commit's message for a `(#M)` squash suffix with M ≠ N (and `gh pr view M --json state` — `MERGED` with the squash on no `origin/*` branch: a squash pushed to a PR head branch exists only on `refs/pull/<n>/head`, so `git branch -r --contains <sha>` comes back empty); state in the review body which parts were already reviewed in M and do not duplicate M's threads.
- **"Tests didn't run locally" in a PR body does not mean untested — CI runs the quick suite inside the `pre-commit` job.** It is a step of the `pre-commit` job in `.github/workflows/code-quality.yml`: `python3 ../apps/predbat/unit_test.py --quick` with `PREDBAT_KERNEL_REQUIRED=1`, so a green pre-commit check has run every TEST_REGISTRY test whose `slow` flag is False — and whether a PR's own new tests exercised in CI depends on that flag, since `--quick` skips `slow=True` modules. Verify with `gh pr view <n> --json statusCheckRollup` (the job's conclusion) plus the touched modules' `slow` flags in `unit_test.py`; cross-repo (fork) PRs get `kernel-binaries` SKIPPED by the workflow's head-repo gate — expected, not a failure (verified 2026-10-18 while reviewing PR #5346).
- **GitNexus is indexed from main, so a PR branch is off-index.** `impact()` returns "Target not found" for PR-only symbols, and `detect_changes()` pins line-shifted hunks on unchanged neighbours - read its output as "which files", not "which symbols", and grep for callers instead (observed reviewing PR #5143). Same session: the review-creation API response reports `comments: 0` even when inline comments attach - verify with `gh api .../reviews/<id>/comments --jq 'length'` before resubmitting.
- **`self.base.args` is process-lifetime, and Predbat writes its own dummy entity ids into it.** `self.args` is assigned once from apps.yaml (`hass.py`, `load_apps_yaml`) and never re-read for the life of the process, while `Inverter.__init__` assigns `create_entity()`'s output — `sensor.{prefix}_{inverter_type}_{id}_{entity_name}` — straight back into `self.base.args[...]`, and **those dummy ids are re-created every cycle even though the Inverter objects themselves now persist**: since PR #5126 (merged 2026-09-26, unreleased at the time of writing) `fetch_inverter_data()` builds the objects only when absent or when `num_inverters` changed, and otherwise calls `refresh_config()`, which re-reads live config and limits every cycle and still re-creates the type-named dummy entities into `self.base.args` — so anything `refresh_config()` samples from live entity state is re-sampled per cycle, not init-only, while the accumulated state `__init__` owns (the commit-once ledger `last_committed`/`commit_pending`) survives — exactly what the pre-#5126 rebuild used to wipe, which is why the committed-schedule guard never suppressed a repeated Solis/GS commit (GH#4712/#2328). **Pre-#5126 logs keep the older reading** — objects rebuilt from scratch every 5-minute cycle (`create=True` default, whole list cleared and re-created each run), so anything init-only was re-sampled per cycle (GH#5099; the pre-#5149 balance-path construction this sentence used to describe is gone — `balance_inverters()` is a pure function since PR #5149 and builds no `Inverter` objects). So with the dummy re-creation running every cycle, any "is this arg configured?" test reading `self.base.args` is reading what Predbat itself wrote last cycle — and the dummy id contains a `.`, so it passes `is_entity_id()` (PR #4745, issue #4738: a custom `GROWATTSPH` type's real `time.*` entity was overwritten by a Predbat dummy). What is lost if a guard then skips recreation on rebuild: `created_attributes` resets in `__init__` and is repopulated only by `create_entity()`, so a skipped sensor-domain write silently drops `state_class: measurement`; and because the id embeds `inverter_type`, a dummy surviving a type change names the **old** type and strands writes on a stale sensor (reproduced in a test on the PR). The obvious alternative authority, `args_from_apps_yaml`, does not generalise here: components write these same args into live `self.args` and never into the snapshot, so keying off the snapshot treats an auto-discovered inverter's select entities as unconfigured. What distinguishes them is recognising Predbat's own naming or a registry of ids created by `create_entity()` — the guard is now on main (`user_configured_entity()`, inverter.py, PR #4745 merged 2026-10-08: a genuinely-wired apps.yaml entity beats the dummy for the four time entities, per the HA write/verify row).
- **Version drift.** Compare `git describe --tags` against the version in the first few lines of their log. A fair number of reports are already fixed on main, and that is a useful triage answer on its own. Two tag quirks: the `v9.0.1` and `v8.55.1` tags alias the **same commit** (`b8996659`), so "diff vs 8.55.1" is the right comparison for any v9.0.1 upgrade report from ≤v8.55.x; and PR #4991's moderate clock-skew warning postdated them both until v9.0.2 (`b8f3f956`, 2026-09-10) shipped it — a "no moderate warning" report is a pre-v9.0.2 version, not a broken feature. A third quirk (GH#5178): a `predbat.log` is **appended across add-on upgrades**, so a log attached to an issue can span two versions — one reporter's log opened with "version v8.55.0 currently running" while they were actually on v9.1.0, and a *deliberate* mid-log downgrade to A/B two versions leaves the same shape by design (GH#5335: v9.3.3 08:41 → v9.3.1 15:35 within one afternoon, giving a clean ~10-minute-apart same-scenario A/B; rebuild the timeline from the `version vX currently running` banners plus the update-service lines before attributing any behaviour to a version). Date a log by the **last** `currently running` occurrence plus restart markers (`Predbat: Startup predbat`), and cross-check `installed_version` in the debug yaml's `CONFIG_ITEMS` (`update.predbat_version` block). "GivTCP: Inverter N REST GET ... successful" lines come from the component polling REST and do not by themselves prove which Predbat version produced the surrounding lines. A fourth quirk is the **downgrade ABI crash** (GH#5213, verified twice in one log): rolling back to ≤v9.0.x with state written by v9.1.0+ crashes the first plan cycle with `TypeError: must be real number, not list` in `double_array` (`prediction_kernel.py`) — PR #5047 changed export limits from packed floats to `(mode, target, power)` tuples (kernel ABI 5→7) and 9.0.x's `double_array` cannot read them — then the next 5-minute cycle runs clean. Expect one skipped plan cycle on any downgrade path; do not misread it as the user's reported bug or as evidence the old version is broken. And a "worked on 9.x, broke on 9.y" claim is a version question before it is a code question — `git tag --contains` the candidate commits before accepting a regression: GH#5390's 9.3.1-works/9.3.3-broke report could not be a code regression, because the whole car-supervision family ships in v9.3.0 with identical grace constants at v9.3.1 (`git show v9.3.1:apps/predbat/const.py`) and v9.3.4's only family change ran in the user's favour.
- **Reading restarts out of a `predbat.log` (GH#3969, verified against a reporter's 65k-line v8.39.5 log, 2026-10-19 — before trusting any "Predbat keeps restarting" headline).** The `version <X> currently running` banner is a **per-cycle line** from the release check (`github.py`), fired at the top of every `update_pred()` (~5 min) — 209 banners in 15.5 h was only 12 restarts; count restarts from the app re-init signature instead: `Loading secrets from ...` → `Loading apps.yaml` → `Predbat: Startup predbat` (that string lives in each version's own init path) → the staged `Initialising <component> interface` / `LoadMLComponent: Started` lines, spread over minutes — those `Started` lines are per-restart context, not evidence of an add-on restart by themselves. Abrupt external kills interrupt a mid-write line (a "Best export windows" print cut mid-sentence, the next line one 5-minute cycle later); a graceful app reload logs a terminate path (`Predbat terminating`), so a re-init with no terminate line and cut-off output is a kill from outside the app — the trigger itself (config edit, add-on restart, OOM) is never in predbat.log, ask for the AppDaemon/add-on log. And nothing watches `/addon_configs/<slug>/predbat_dashboard.yaml`: the only file-watch-restart in the repo is the **standalone** path (`hass.py` `collect_watch_files` — `*.py` plus the real apps.yaml), which the add-on path doesn't run, so a "the dashboard file restarted me" theory should first have the dashboard writes correlated against restart timestamps (writes also *lag* restarts — a fresh instance rewrites it). Pre-v9 Load ML drift worth the same context (v8.39.x): `_do_training()` ran an initial curriculum session and then an **unconditional second** session with no else, so the first save was 30–90+ minutes away on Pi-4-speed hardware and any restart in that window restarted training from zero; main uses a single if/else plus a `progress_callback` that keeps the component alive, and main restores `last_train_time` from the loaded model (`load_ml_component.py`) where v8.39.5 did not — so every app restart re-fired a full curriculum fine-tune — and "No training needed/fine-tune (2h interval), model age is 2.0 hours" on that era is the `last_train_time is None` sentinel printing the interval default, not a real age; a run of "Starting fine-tune training" lines is one multi-pass curriculum session (`train_curriculum()`'s non-first passes), not repeated trainings.
- **A module's red run proves only its first new test can fail (seen on PR #5641, runner read on main 697c44ca, 2026-10-20).** `run_kraken_tests()` (`tests/test_kraken.py`) runs every `test_*` in alphabetical `dir()` order and **returns on the first failure**, so a stashed-source red run of `tools/triage_test.sh kraken` shows exactly one FAIL and the other new tests never execute — no evidence any of them can fail. What worked under the triage permission mode (where a single-test direct run is denied and only `triage_test.sh` is allowed): rename the test that just failed to `xtest_...`, re-run, repeat until the run is green, revert the renames before `git stash pop` (~0.1 s per kraken run). Read the specific module's runner before trusting a single red FAIL when more than one test was added — the registry loop in `unit_test.py` aborts on a raise (see Test-order pollution above), which is a different "later tests never ran" shape.
- **CI's `python-version: 3.x` can resolve to different CPythons on different runners.** (Verified from job logs, 2026-10-19.) On one day it landed CPython **3.15.0** on three runs across unrelated branches (PR #5642 and two other bot PRs, all failing `test_str2time` identically) and **3.14.8** on another run that passed. Grep the `Successfully set up CPython` line of the job log before blaming a PR for an unrelated failing test — a same-base control run without comparing interpreter versions led one bot review of PR #5642 astray (it blamed "interpreter-state pollution" by the PR's own new test; the real mechanism was CPython 3.15, not interference). The underlying break is fixed on main by PR #5653: from Python 3.15 `strptime` returns a naive datetime instead of raising when a `%z` format meets a string with no offset, and `strptime_aware()` (`utils.py`) now raises whenever the parse has no tzinfo — `str2time_strptime()` and the production `%z` strptime sites go through it (nothing changes on ≤3.14; the variable-format inverter clock loop is deliberately left outside, it localises a naive result itself), with an AST-scan test (Test 6 in `test_str2time.py`) stopping new direct `strptime` calls from reintroducing the gap. Review finding on the fix (still open on main): `offset_strptime_calls()` resolves the format only when the argument is a string literal or a plain `Name` in its known-map — an `Attribute` form (`const.TIME_FORMAT`) or a `BinOp` composite (`"%Y-" + CONST`, the shape `utils.py` itself uses) silently passes, so a future `datetime.strptime(x, const.TIME_FORMAT)` gets a green build out of the guard; matching `ast.Attribute` nodes with `.attr` in the known-map is the cheap patch.
- **`timedelta.seconds` wraps into [0, 86399] — settled at the button-press site, and there is a check before triaging any other `.seconds` site.** Fixed in PR #5697 (merged 2026-10-24; inverter.py now writes `abs((now_local - time_pressed).total_seconds()) < 10` with the comment citing #5694) — keep the pre-#5697 signature for older logs, where a stamp slightly ahead of Predbat's clock read ~86399 s stale and failed all 10 press retries, then fed `record_commit(False)` so the commit re-pressed every cycle (GH#5694), and a multi-day-old stamp read as just-seconds-fresh. Before triaging any remaining `age.seconds` comparison as a wrap bug, check whether a cleanup pass caps the age below 24 h at that site: gecloud (`clean_ge_url_cache()` deletes >24 h entries using `total_seconds()`, read-verified) and futurerate (`clean_futurerate_cache()`) both do, so their `.seconds` checks are correct as written — and `futurerate.py`'s `update_time_since_midnight.seconds < 0` is dead twice over (a normalised `.seconds` is never negative, and the same cleanup deletes yesterday's entries) — do not put it in a sweep ticket. The idiom for any future site is `abs((a - b).total_seconds())`, and with `strptime_aware()` (#5653) the parsed side is aware, so the defect at such sites is the normalisation, not tz confusion.
- **Recognising dead vs alive naive-tz code after `strptime_aware()` (GH#5695 — the octopus.py/solcast.py dead sites were removed on main by PR #5696, merged 2026-10-23; this is the checklist for the next sweep).** `strptime_aware()` (utils.py) raises whenever the parse carries no tzinfo, on every Python version, so: (1) a `%z`-format site still guarding/normalising a naive result is dead — the naive case now raises inside `strptime_aware()` and is dropped by the surrounding except; (2) a `.replace(tzinfo=...)` written as a bare statement is a no-op dead, and that shape predates #5653 (a discarded value), while `.replace(...)` chained onto an assignment is alive — check the assignment before sweeping; (3) naive-format strptime sites are not covered by the #5653 AST guard (it pins `%z` formats only) and are mostly functional — the inverter clock format scan is the deliberate naive-result loop (it localises the naive result itself). Deleting a dead branch orphans things flake8 catches only at commit time: locals whose only use was in the branch and module imports whose only use was the dead line (F401).
- **Replay-probe traps when A/B-ing a plan from a debug yaml (GH#5616, two reruns' worth, 2026-10-18).** (1) Filtering export windows "after 19:00" as `start >= 1140` deletes **tomorrow's** windows too — the dump's window list is one minute-of-day frame holding both days — so filter by the concrete range (`1140 <= start < 1440`) or by end time as well, or the replay metric collapses by hundreds of p and looks like a bug. (2) The simulated SoC is baked at `Prediction(...)` construction — mutating `soc_kw` afterwards never reaches the simulation (two variants scored byte-identical to baseline); to probe "what if the battery were fuller", vary the window/limit arrays or rebuild the `Prediction` object. Also a tag-availability trap when version-matching: `git describe --tags` after `git fetch origin main` still shows the wrong version until tags are fetched too — `git tag --contains HEAD` answers "is HEAD in release X" directly.
- **A/B replaying a dump across versions: check out the old tag in place (GH#5719, settled a "did the update change this" question without user evidence).** `git checkout -q v9.3.8`, then `cd coverage && ./run_all --debug_file <dump> --redo`, on both trees — `git worktree add` is denied under the triage permission mode, plain checkout is not (mind the `manifest.yaml` trap above before the first run). The identity of the two metrics only has force if the tag-to-tag diff is inert for the affected config — read it first (GH#5719's v9.3.8→main delta: 109 lines of plan.py + 9 of prediction.py, all Octopus-Intelligent car logic, inert with `num_cars` 0). And when the dump is fresh (captured hours before attachment), check replay-vs-reporter-log agreement first: the replay's plan rows matched the live log row-for-row, which validates the replay before anything is built on it.
- **Ad-hoc dump replay under the triage permission mode.** Env-var prefixes and `sh <script>` in front of `tools/triage_test.sh` break the literal-command allowlist; the shape that worked (GH#5633 replay): hardcode the dump path in a temporary probe at the top of `run_debug_cases()` (`unit_test.py`), trigger it via a marker file in scratch (env vars cannot be passed), gate every harness patch on the env/marker so the golden cases run untouched, then `git checkout --` + `git clean -f` afterwards. And the plan's own pass/debug lines (`Try optimising export window(s) ...`, `Reverted change to d window ...`) land in `coverage/predbat.log`, **not** in the test log the wrapper writes — grep there before concluding a pass never ran.
- **A component's control events can run on a throwaway event loop (Hanchu; fixed on main by PR #5684, 2026-10-21, reproduced end to end with a real `aiohttp` session — kept as the class signpost for the next component that keeps a session or lock in `self` and does I/O directly in its event handler).** When Predbat's plan thread presses a component's entity (a schedule-write switch, a select), the press reaches `select_event`/`switch_event`/`number_event` through `ha.py`'s loopback `call_service` → `run_async(trigger_callback(...))`, which runs it with `asyncio.run` on a fresh loop. Anything bound to the component's own loop then breaks there: a persistent `aiohttp.ClientSession` raises `Timeout context manager should be used inside a task` (wrapped by a broad `except` it looks like a transport failure), and an `asyncio.Lock` stops serialising across the two loops. The symptom is a write that fails on every plan cycle and lands a minute later from the component's own `run()` tick. Look for a component that keeps a session or lock in `self` *and* does I/O directly in its event handler. Per-request sessions (alphaess, sunsynk, deye, fox, sigenergy) sidestep it. `gateway.py` `_publish_raw()` and Hanchu `_on_component_loop()` hand the work over with `run_coroutine_threadsafe` onto the loop recorded in `run()`.
- **Log noise.** `predbat.log` carries routine `Warn:` lines (config clamps, kernel status, unsupported settings). Don't quote a warning as the root cause unless it lines up with the time the reporter describes.
- **HA-side service/entity warnings never appear in `predbat.log`.** Lines like `Referenced entities switch.predbat_set_reserve_enable are missing or not currently available` (logger `homeassistant.helpers.service`, `Source: helpers/target.py:152`) are written by HA core around any app's service calls and land in the **HA core log** - an AppDaemon app's log carries none of them (Predbat's own lines are `Note:`/`Warn:`/info style; on GH#5420 the attached 50k-line `predbat.log` grepped 0 for the string and holds no ERROR/WARNING lines at all). On a startup-race/entity-not-ready report, predbat.log shows what Predbat *did* per cycle; ask for the HA core log for the not-available text instead of waiting for it inside predbat.log.
- **Hardware questions.** A unit test settles what the code does, not what an inverter did. Several findings here were only confirmed on live hardware. If the question is hardware behaviour, say the maintainer needs to confirm it rather than running a test to look thorough.
- **`gh issue list --search` without `--repo` searches all of GitHub.** Confident-looking hits can be from unrelated projects (GH#4705). Always pass `--repo springfall2008/batpred` on a duplicate search. An **empty** result deserves the same suspicion as a confident one, and usually the cause is the query rather than the machinery: a triage run reported `gh search issues` as dead on the runner because a search for GH#4961's title text returned nothing, when that issue's real title misspells "unreasonably" - the run had silently corrected the typo while retyping the title, and searching the spelling the reporter actually used finds it (GH#4973, re-probed). Fetch the issue's exact title rather than retyping it, and sanity-check an empty result with a distinctive token from a known issue before reporting no duplicates. Before wording a split-issue request for a multi-symptom report, run the same search against the *reporter's own* issues too - GH#5420's second symptom (reserve=100 on Tesla TBC) already had its own ticket (GH#5300, same reporter), so the right ask was to continue on #5300 rather than open a new issue.
- **Issue-linked branch docs are read with `git fetch`, not `gh api` (GH#5291, verified 2026-09-28).** Issue bodies produced by the bot's own PR flow can reference plan docs living on `claude/*` branches (typically under `docs/superpowers/plans/`); the triage permission mode denies `gh api .../contents?ref=...` but keeps the broad `git` grant, so `git fetch origin <branch>` + `git show <branch>:<path>` is the route that works. And **Kraken (EDF/E.ON) issues carry no component label** — #4157 has none at all and #5166 has only `bug`/`priority_medium`/`BOT_TRIAGED`/`BOT_PR_OPENED` — the repo has an `Octopus` label but no `kraken`/`edf`/`eon` one and prior Kraken issues were not given `Octopus`, so skip the component label there rather than guessing one (verified 2026-09-28 by reading the label sets). And `gh issue view --json comments --jq length` on an issue with no comments returns the *object's* key count, not 0 — jq's context is the wrapper `{comments: [...]}`, so plain `length` counts its keys; write `--jq '.comments | length'` (GH#5349).
- **Verifying a claim that lives in another repo (GH#5417).** `gh api repos/<other-org>/<repo>/contents/...` is denied by the triage runner's permission mode (same denial class as the PR-review-comment fetch in the HA write/verify row) — don't retry it, WebFetch is the route. And WebFetch on `raw.githubusercontent.com/.../main/...` 404s when that repo's default branch isn't `main`: fetch the repo's landing page first — it answers "what is the default branch" directly — then fetch the raw file on that branch. Verified on GH#5417: the third-party EDF integration repo cited by the issue has `develop` as its default branch (the landing page answers that question directly) and the raw file on `develop` returned the quoted material in full.
- **`gh api` flag order decides whether the call is allowed (PR #5759 round, verified live 2026-10-26).** `gh api --paginate <url> --jq '...'` (flags before the endpoint) is denied while the identical call with the flags after the endpoint works — the permission rules match a literal command prefix, so re-order flags before concluding a call is unaffordable (and a quoted URL with a query string can be denied in the same way as the flag-first form). With events, `GET /repos/O/R/issues/N/events` returns **oldest-first** (confirmed by `created_at` across one issue's 9 events), so "latest" selection is tail-based (`logins[-1]`) — keep that in mind for any "latest label event" lookup. When hunting "is there already a PR for this issue", the daemon-style exact-phrase search understates: `"Fixes #5079" in:body` returned `[]` while the bare-token `Fixes #5079 in:body` found #5097/#5126, and PR #5449 says "Addresses the start-up half of #5134" — search bare `#<n> in:body` too. And a `BOT_PR` labeled event can be authored by the bot's own credential, so requester-linking must tolerate the bot-as-requester (assigning the repo owner no-ops against `/issue-pr`'s default assignee).
- **A flat SoC reading isn't necessarily a stuck charge.** `soc_kw` is derived from `soc_percent × soc_max / 100` (`inverter.py:1422-1428`); on a large pack, one percentage point is a meaningful chunk of energy — e.g. ~0.32 kWh on a 31.6 kWh pack, ~9.5 minutes flat at a 2 kW rate. Before treating a flat run as evidence of a stall, check whether its duration is fully explained by the pack's percent-to-kWh granularity at the charge/discharge rate in play (GH#4784).
- **The HA Companion app's embedded webview ignores `Content-Disposition: attachment`** and renders any download link inline instead, even when the server sends correct headers — confirmed for both the debug-snapshot and bulk-archive routes (GH#4720). A "download doesn't work" report from the Companion app specifically is a client limitation, not a server bug. The advice this bullet used to carry - that the snapshots sit as plain-text `.txt` YAML in `<config root>/cache/` - is now out of date twice over. PR #4743 (GH#4720) moved the periodic snapshots into `debug/` under their real capture name, and PR #4941 (GH#4932) renamed that to `predbat_debug_<id>.yaml.txt`, because **GitHub rejects a bare `.yaml` attachment** - which is the entire reason for the trailing `.txt` (`snapshot_filename()`, `debug_history.py:219`; `_legacy_snapshot_filename()` still resolves and prunes pre-upgrade `.yaml` files for the rest of their time in the ring). So a reporter can now attach a ring snapshot straight out of `debug/` with no renaming. `create_debug_yaml()`'s separate per-cycle dump is still written as a bare `debug/predbat_debug_HH_MM_SS.yaml` (`userinterface.py:761`) and does still have to be renamed before it can be attached. The bulk archive has the mirror-image constraint and must stay **compressed** end to end: browsers that unarchive downloads do it by extension, and a 15-snapshot history is tens of MB of yaml against a few MB as a tarball (32 MB vs 4.6 MB when measured on GH#4898's smaller install; the current case files measure ~5 MB per snapshot, so 15 ≈ 75 MB) - unpacked it breaks GitHub's 25 MB attachment limit *and* GitHub rejects bare `.tar` outright, while `.tgz` is accepted (PR #4898). Sizes are install-dependent, so re-measure before doing storage arithmetic on them. The ~10x compression also hides a live cost: `html_debug_history_download_recent` (`web.py`; named `html_debug_history_download_all` and uncapped before PR #5071) loads the newest snapshots' full text, re-encodes each string and gzips the lot into one in-memory `BytesIO`, synchronously inside the aiohttp handler — no streaming, so peak RSS ≈ 2x the archive's uncompressed corpus. Since PR #5071 (merged 2026-09-19) the route is capped at the `DEBUG_HISTORY_DOWNLOAD_MAX` (16) newest snapshots while `debug_history_count`'s max rose from 50 to 500 (#5070/#5071); the uncapped every-snapshot version is pre-#5071 only.
