- **Two scans over one slot must agree on their range.** *(Both halves below were fixed in PR #4943; the mechanism is kept because it recurs and because an older log still shows it.)* `calculate_yesterday()` rebuilds each History slot with two walks over the same per-minute status list: one decides *whether* the slot rebuilds a charge/export window, the other decides *where* that window starts. When #4872 widened only the first to trust homogeneous slot edges, a state living solely in a trusted edge was counted but never given a start minute, so the window was appended as `{"start": None, "end": None}` and `in_charge_window()` raised `TypeError: '>=' not supported between instances of 'int' and 'NoneType'` — killing the whole `update_pred()` cycle, not just the History view (regression from PR #4872). A `TypeError` comparing against `None` deep in the plan is worth tracing back to a *pair* of loops that drifted apart. The same edit uncovered a second defect in those loops: the charge/export handoff ("whichever side starts second ends the one that started first") also fired when both sides started at the *same* minute, which is what Cross-charging is - so every cross-charging slot rebuilt a **zero-width** export window (`start == end`), invisible to `in_charge_window()` and worth nothing in the simulated cost. The export half of cross-charging was thus still missing from History long after #4466 "restored" it, because `_test_cross_charging_reconstructed_as_both_windows` only asserted the window list was non-empty. **A window assertion that does not check `start < end` proves nothing** - a list of zero-width windows passes it.

- **Two traps when adding a guard to an `Execute` entry point** (found writing the GH#4965 fix, merged as PR #4968). `test_control_ledger.py`'s `test_every_entry_point_opens_its_own_cycle` builds a bare `Execute.__new__(Execute)` stub (`test_control_ledger.py:1845`) and calls `quick_inverter_data_update()` on it directly, so any new base-class read added to that function fails the quick suite with `'Execute' object has no attribute 'get_arg'`. The stub now carries a `get_arg` lambda, but the shape is the trap: the same is true of every entry point tested this way. The second is worth more than the first - **the harness cannot reproduce the crash the issue describes**. Deleting `inverter_clock_skew_discharge_start` and calling the unguarded function does *not* raise, because that read (`inverter_clock_skew_discharge_start`, applied inside the `"discharge_start_time" in self.base.args` branch) sits behind that membership check, which the mock inverter never satisfies. Verified by stashing the fix and re-running `./run_all --test execute`: the regression test failed on its return-value assertion, not on the AttributeError branch it was written for. For this class of init-order bug, assert that the guard is present and returns early; an attribute-deletion reproduction passes silently whether or not the bug is fixed.
- **Building a component through its real constructor in a test aliases `self.args` to the live shared args** (found reviewing PR #5211's Apps-tab filter box). `ComponentBase.__init__` sets `self.args = base.args` — the same object the running fixture uses — and then runs `initialize(**kwargs)`, so a component constructed with `__init__` in a test is bound to the live shared args until a later line rebinds it, and silently renders whatever the shared fixture happens to hold if that rebinding is dropped or reordered. `WebInterface.initialize()` additionally constructs `AnnualPage` and `WebChat`, none of which a single-page test needs. The established pattern (`_reset_fixture`, `tests/test_web_apps_edit.py`) is `WebInterface.__new__(WebInterface)` plus explicit `base`/`log`/`prefix`/`args` — `html_apps()` needs exactly those four (it assigns `self.default_page` itself on entry). Generalises to all 18 components; same family as the `Execute.__new__` stub trap above from the other side (the stub misses base methods, the real constructor brings too much with it). Whether any currently-registered test actually mis-renders because of this is unaudited — suspected, not verified.
- **Check that a report's premise exists before verifying its logic.** Three reports in two weeks were built on configuration or history that is not there: GH#4918 quoted `tesla_tariff_sync`, `tesla_smart_plan` and `tesla_smart_plan_max_export`, none of which appear anywhere in the codebase or `APPS_SCHEMA` (an AI assistant invented them for the reporter); GH#4917 treated `trust_future_dynamic_iog_slots` as shipped when it exists only on the closed-unmerged PR #4885 - on `main`, `rate_add_io_slots()` trusts every planned IOG slot unconditionally (pre-#5403 reading; since PR #5403 the `octopus_intelligent_consider_full` car-need gate withholds car-needless *future* dispatch minutes, though the current half hour still stamps — see the GH#5424 row); and GH#5022 (an enhancement issue written by Claude under the reporter's account) claims #5015 "turned off `support_feedin_first` for FoxCloud", which `git log -S` disproves — the flag was set once by `3ed9151e` and never removed. Grep `config.py` / `APPS_SCHEMA` for every setting a report names, and check `git log` or `gh pr view` before accepting an "added for #NNNN" claim — including the background section of an issue you are about to implement. The same check covers gating premises: GH#5237's issue body claimed the car-planning mitigations (`car_charging_plan_smart`, `car_charging_plan_max_price`, `best_soc_keep`) are "expert gated", false on both v9.0.3 and main — the two car settings carry `enable: num_cars` only and `best_soc_keep` has no gate at all — so a docs change premised on it would have been wasted; start any "setting hidden behind expert mode" claim with the item's `enable:` field in `config.py`. The same check runs in the other direction: an enhancement proposal can describe symbols that live only on the PR branch it was filed from — GH#5063 proposed hardening a log-redaction invalidation cache that existed only on the then-open PR #5053 and not on main (it has since merged, and PR #5171 has since moved the cache into the `LogRedaction` mixin (`log_secrets.py`) — so the cache it describes is real on current main), and overstated its own audit (production direct mutations of `self.args` are ~6 sites in 5 files — `chat_tools.py`, `create_missing_arg()` in `inverter.py`, `userinterface.py`, the one bulk `clear()+update()` in `web.py`, `web_chat.py` — not "roughly 15 files"; the deye/fox/teslemetry grep hits are `__main__` CLI blocks writing `mock_base`).
- **Cross-check an attached debug yaml against the attached log before believing either (GH#5335).** Reporters sometimes attach someone else's `predbat_debug.yaml` — picked up from a community thread, and the issue's own opening comment may say so ("the debug shows sensor X which isn't my sensor"). At the yaml's capture minute, compare its state dump (`soc_kw`/`soc_max`) and its resolved entity serials (the "Regular expression argument" lines) against what the reporter's own log says at that timestamp: a different SoC, different Zappi/GE serials or Octopus account ids mean the yaml is foreign, only the log is then usable, and the reporter's own yaml must be re-asked for before config-dependent conclusions. The yaml's config can also flatly contradict the issue body (a direct zappi sensor named where the body says a regex) — the file wins over the prose, but only once it has been proven to be theirs.
- **Attachment forensics: numbered logs, rolling snapshots and download-time dumps disagree (GH#5344).** Numbered AppDaemon logs (`predbat.1.log`, `predbat.2.log`) are download-time snapshots that overlap, not rotation order — one issue attached `.1.log` with the issue body (ending Oct 1 18:30) and `.2.log` in a follow-up comment (running Oct 1 00:00 → Oct 2 06:44), so the lower number was the *older* archive; check head+tail timestamps of every numbered log before building a timeline across them. The rolling debug-history tarball's newest snapshot is a ~3-hourly capture and can miss the triggering event entirely (there the newest still predated the ~18:00 IOG dispatch arrival the reporter described); the single `predbat_debug.yaml` is download-time state, not complaint-time state; and each snapshot is the state at *its* capture minute as well (see the GH#5335 bullet above). Within one log tick of the plan log, a repeated `Best charge window`/`Raw charge windows` pair is the retained plan re-logged plus the 5-minute recompute (`Recompute is saving previous plan...`, plan.py) — not two plans running in parallel, and the retained-first ordering means the *later* line is the newer plan, not the earlier one. **And `Best charge window` is the prefilled incumbent, never the optimised plan** (GH#5434): the recompute block resets `charge_window_best` from `low_rates`/`charge_window` and runs `prefill_charge_limit_best()` before the line prints (plan.py), so it shows the incumbent limits carried onto the reset windows; the optimiser's output only appears in the later `Raw charge windows` / `Unclipped/Unfiltered/Filtered charge windows` lines, and the adoption decision is its own pair of lines after a metric comparison of both plans re-scored on the same cycle's inputs (`run_prediction_metric()` for each) — comparing the Best print against the writes that follow misreads which line is the plan. GitHub user-attachment behaviour (observed twice, GH#5503, 2026-10-12): a freshly uploaded attachment's URL can 404 for the first ~10+ minutes, and re-attaching mints **new** ids rather than reviving the old URLs — retry the original ids once, then ask the reporter to re-attach instead of waiting the hour it took the originals to come alive.
- **`grid_power` is negative when importing.** That is the repo's convention: `docs/faq.md` states it, `web.py`'s power-flow code reads it that way, and `test_enphase_api.py` asserts it by name - `grid_power_invert` exists precisely for meters that do the opposite. The outlier is `predbat_metrics.py:101`'s Prometheus help string, which claims "positive for import". GH#4797 read that string as authoritative and concluded the web arrows and icons were inverted; measured against every other statement of the convention, they are not. Work out which side of that contradiction a sign report is arguing from before agreeing with it.
- **Docs yaml code fences are unvalidated (GH#5479).** Nothing validates the fenced yaml script blocks in `docs/inverter-setup.md`: pre-commit's `check-yaml` hook only matches `.yaml`/`.yml` files and markdownlint does not parse fence content, so a docs-only commit can ship invalid yaml that every reader pastes. On a "this doc pasted script breaks NOW" report, extract each fence with sed and `yaml.safe_load` it - two SolaX script blocks (modes 1 and 8) shipped broken since Feb 2026, caused not by an integration edit but by PR #3389, a LuxPower-section re-indent ~200 lines away that re-indented the `- variables:`/`- action:` lines without shifting their nested blocks. Two break shapes: `data:`/`target:` aligned with the `- action:` dash → hard parse error; variable keys at the same indent as `variables:` → **silently parseable** (`variables: null`, the keys become junk step keys), so a partially-fixed copy loads but loses its variables. `git blame -L <fence range>` on the fence and suspect any wide reformatting commit, not only integration-expertise edits (the sibling semantic-docs-defect class: GH#5459, fix in flight on PR #5464).
- **A passing test proves nothing when its fixture encodes the bug.** This has now happened often enough to be a check rather than a surprise: `web_power_flow` shares the sign assumption it would have to catch; the `iboost_smart` fixture uses a flat rate, so a rate-averaging bug cannot show; `test_balance_inverters` configures two *identical* inverters, so neither the capacity-blind SoC split nor the 3+ inverter partner ring is exercised (that was pre-#5149; the balance rework added `test_balance_pure.py`, which covers the SoC branches, cross-charge and pause semantics - and GH#4856 remains the canonical catalogue of balancer-design observations (ring partner selection, bang-bang control, equal-SoC% targets, the throttle-only surface): link to it rather than re-deriving them, and note pre-rework `balance_inverters()` line references are meaningless now that it is a pure function in `utils.py` driven from `rebalance_inverter_rates()`); PR #4935 found a cap test whose slot started after the window it was capping against, so the loop under test ran zero iterations; and GH#4826's `write_failure` test mocks a response with no error code, leaving the classification branch untested. `TestHAInterface.get_state` (test_infra.py) is its own instance of the same lesson: it returns `""` - not the caller's `default` - when a queried *attribute* is missing, while production `ha.py` returns the default, so a test written against the mock can be vacuous for default-handling when both values land in the same branch (found reviewing PR #5131). Read the fixture before quoting "the test passes" in a comment - and say what the test would have to look like to catch the reported behaviour. A sibling shape: **an allocator probe cannot test an identity-keyed cache guard** (GH#5171 review). The intuitive regression test for the `id()`-reuse bug in `LogRedaction._log_secret_fingerprint()` — free the mapping, allocate a batch of same-shaped dicts, assert none lands on the recorded address — **passed against the unfixed code** (64 decoy dicts after `gc.collect()`, no decoy ever landed there), because whether CPython recycles a freed dict's address depends on allocator pool state. What works deterministically is asserting the property that *rules the collision out*: the stored fingerprint still refers to the mapping it describes, so the recorded address belongs to a live object and cannot be recycled at all — `any(ref is stored for ref in gc.get_referrers(superseded_args))` (dicts are not weakref-able, so `gc.get_referrers()` is the tool); `test_log_secret_fingerprint_is_not_fooled_by_address_reuse()` in `tests/test_secrets.py` is the in-repo precedent, and the fix itself (hold the mappings, compare with `is`, not `==`) is merged (`7d17ea1b`). Generalises to any guard whose correctness rests on "this address still means what it meant": test that the object is pinned, not that the allocator declined to reuse the address. Same class: `test_kraken.py`'s `_mk_get_ctx` sets `return_value=json_body or {}`, so a falsy JSON body (`[]`/`None`) silently becomes a valid empty dict and the test passes for the wrong reason — build the mock context, then set its `.json` return explicitly when the case needs a non-object body (PR #5641).
- **A test whose strong assertions sit behind an import-availability gate never runs them in CI or the coverage venv (GH#5715, verified 2026-10-24 by reading both gated environments).** CI (`code-quality.yml`: `pip install -r requirements.txt`, then `unit_test.py --quick`) and `coverage/setup.csh` (same, into `coverage/venv`) install **requirements.txt and nothing else**, so a test's `if have_crypto:`-style gate on a lazily-imported package skips its assertion half everywhere gated — green CI proves the skip-path ran, not the assertions. Live instance: PR #5715's Hanchu AES known-answer vectors for `encrypt_payload` sit behind `cryptography`, which is deliberately not in requirements.txt (a CLI-harness-only import). The honest fix is installing the package into the test venv (`coverage/setup.csh`), not requirements.txt; check any future optional-codec/wire-format/vendored-crypto test's import gate the same way (sigenergy/solis-style lazy imports).
- **A negative log-grep assertion is dead when the method whose log line it greps is one of the harness's silent no-op stubs (GH#5718, verified in `test_ge_cloud.py`).** Pre-merge, `_make_rediscovery_ge_cloud()` stubbed `update_site()` as a benign no-op while a test "proved" `update_site()` is not called in evc-only mode by grepping `log_messages` for "Site details" — which only `update_site()` emits — so the assertion was empty by construction and a regression un-gating `update_site()` would pass green. The merged #5718 routes it through the `calls["site"]` counter instead (in-repo precedent). Applies to any component test harness built on a stub dict + silent no-ops: check each negative assertion's log string against the stub set before treating it as verified, and prefer routing the behaviour through the calls dict.
- **One unexplained observation, now explained and fixed.** In GH#4900's log, at 00:00:27, the nightly tariff-compare scenario for `igo_fixed` - running under swapped flat 12.0p export rates - had its scenario plan accepted as the **live** plan (`using new plan` logged from inside the scenario). A recalculation 18 seconds later replaced it, so nothing was harmed that night; the code path was not identified within triage scope at the time. It later was: the compare scenario's `calculate_plan()` replaced `plan_preclip` (which plan selection scores as the plan in force) and `run_all()` restored the windows and limits but not the pre-clip copy — so the first re-plan after a midnight comparison scored the last tariff's plan as the incumbent and could switch regardless of `metric_min_improvement_plan` on a badly-scored previous plan (PR #5465, `dcc6017d`, 2026-10-12, which restores the saved plan state in a `finally`). On current main a report of "the midnight compare changed my plan" maps here; `using new plan` inside a compare scenario on a pre-#5465 log is this mechanism.
- **Never run the test harness in a fresh git worktree without copying `manifest.yaml` in first.** Importing `predbat.py` runs `check_install()` (`download.py`). `apps/predbat/manifest.yaml` is gitignored, so a new worktree has none: Predbat fetches the manifest for `THIS_VERSION` from GitHub, finds the branch's file list does not match (a file the release lacks, such as `log_secrets.py`, is enough), then downloads the release and **overwrites the worktree's tracked files** with it - including whatever you just edited - and exits. Seen on 24 Sep 2026: ten files, the kernel `.so` binaries among them, reverted to v9.2.0 in one run. The only sign in the log is `Predbat files are not installed correctly, trying to download them`. Copy the main checkout's `manifest.yaml` into the worktree before the first run; if it has already happened, `git checkout -- .` and `git clean -fd apps/predbat` in the worktree, then redo your edits.
- **The quick suite fails 00:00-01:00 local on unmodified main** (observed 2026-10-14 00:11-00:15 BST by five consecutive `./run_all --quick` runs): `test_teslemetry.py::test_teslemetry_local_weekday_follows_the_base_clock` asserts `api._local_today_weekday() == datetime.now().weekday()` with `api.base = None`, but the fallback is `datetime.now(self.local_tz or timezone.utc)` (teslemetry.py) and `MockTeslemetryAPI` has no `local_tz`, so the fallback is UTC — the assertion fails whenever the local calendar day is ahead of UTC's (00:00-01:00 in a UTC+1 zone), which is the same local-vs-UTC weekday disagreement the function's own docstring warns about. The registry loop has no try/except (see Test-order pollution above), so this one raise aborts every later test: a `--quick` run started in that window dies silently at teslemetry with a bare traceback and no FAILED/summary line. Check the wall clock before re-running a suite failure at those times; a rerun an hour later goes green.
- **`docs/apps-yaml.md` documents each setting twice — and only the bullet is where a key grep looks.** A config key gets a `**key** -` bullet in the CONFIG reference (e.g. `inverter_reserve_max`) and a separate `### <Display Name>` detail section further down (`### Inverter reserve maximum`), and the cross-page links point at the **detail** section's anchor (`docs/inverter-setup.md` links `#inverter-reserve-maximum`), so a docs fix that updates only the bullet leaves the section a user is actually linked to carrying the old guidance — and a grep of the config key misses it, because the section heading is the display name: search the display name too, or list the page's `###` sections. Live instance: the bullet says "(default is 100)" while the linked-to section still says "Global, sets the maximum reserve % that may be set to the inverter, the default is 98" (found reviewing PR #5644, 2026-10-20; still on main — grep `docs/` for the display name before calling a docs fix done).
- **Docs entity names that live in HA core, not this repo (GH#5755, verified 2026-10-25 against main + HA core's `hassio` integration).** `binary_sensor.predbat_running` in the Error Monitor template (docs/output-data.md) is not a Predbat entity — zero occurrences in `apps/predbat` or in code history — it is the Home Assistant **Supervisor (hassio) per-add-on "Running" binary sensor**, one per add-on, entity_id derived from the add-on *display name*, and **disabled by default** (`entity_registry_enabled_default=False`), so it is absent from states until a user enables it manually. With no Supervisor (HA Container/Core or the Docker product) or no add-on it genuinely does not exist — delete the template trigger. The same mechanism explains `binary_sensor.appdaemon_running` and the old `binary_sensor.givtcp_running` placeholders; `docs/install.md`'s monitor step tells users to enable it but the Error Monitor section never repeats that, which is what the issue tripped over. Trap: don't grep `apps/predbat` for a docs' binary_sensor name and conclude "entity doesn't exist, docs wrong" — supervisor-created add-on entities live entirely in HA core.
- **Re-running `/pr-review` on an unchanged PR head duplicates the previous bot review.** The skill has no dedup step and GitHub accepts repeated reviews at the same commit, so a second run posts duplicate inline comments at the same anchors: at the start of a review run, read the PR's existing reviews (`gh pr view <n> --json reviews`) and the inline threads before reviewing; if the head commit is unchanged since the last bot review, re-verify the earlier findings against the checkout (the fresh anchor reads are what makes restating legitimate) and post a follow-up review that restates/links them with `comments: []` — if the head moved, review normally and anchor only in the new diff. If a cleanup fix exists locally but is unpushed (blocked), say so in the review body so the maintainer knows why nothing changed (PR #5645 re-review, 2026-10-20).
- **Five standing conventions when reviewing an inverter-template PR (Indra/Hanchu class; one wrong candidate wasted on PR #5648, Indra V2H, 2026-10-20).** (a) Sign conventions: `grid_power` is **negative when importing, positive when exporting** and `battery_power` is **positive while discharging, negative while charging** (until `battery_power_invert` flips it), so a charge-positive EV-power sensor (Indra's `power_to_ev`) correctly takes `battery_power_invert: True` — check which direction the device's sensor counts as positive before judging an invert flag. (b) An unknown `inverter_type` needs **no code change**: `INVERTER_DEF[type]` is synthesised from a GE copy with `rate_step_percent_of_capacity` stripped, then every key of the template's `inverter:` dict is overlaid — so a new brand works as long as the block sets each key the read block uses (`test_custom_inverter_def_drops_rate_step` pins it). (c) `battery_min_soc` reaches the plan only through the inverter reserve chain — it floors `reserve_min`/`reserve_percent` (`inverter.py`), then the plan sees `reserve = soc_max × reserve_percent/100` — so grepping only plan.py/prediction.py/predbat.py for it returns nothing and wrongly suggests a docs bullet about it is inert; its other consumers are components binding it to device reserve entities. (d) The freeze-export fallback **assumes `discharge_stop_service` is a neutral hold**: with no `discharge_freeze_service` configured, `adjust_export_immediate(freeze=True)` falls back to `discharge_stop_service` (`inverter.py`, whose comment assumes a genuine freeze), so on a mode-select device whose stop mapping is an active mode (Indra → LOAD_MATCH) the "freeze" actively discharges — check both `{charge,discharge}_freeze_service` wirings when reviewing mode-select templates. (e) A `template:` sensor's entity_id derives from the slug of its `name:`, never from `unique_id:` — so cross-check every apps.yaml entity reference in a template PR against the `name:` lines of the template sensors the PR asks the user to create; only two of four matched in the Indevolt walkthrough (GH#5683, caught reviewing that pending template PR on 2026-10-23), and punctuation in a name can double up underscores in the entity id. A mismatched reference reads unavailable/zero with only a Predbat Warn, and the whole model rests on it (`soc_kw` included). (f) **Verify every new `mdi:` icon name against the upstream SVG** (`raw.githubusercontent.com/MaterialDesign-SVG/master/svg/<name>.svg`; under the triage permission mode WebFetch on that URL works where plain `curl` is denied — control with a definitely-real sibling icon so a site-wide outage is not misread as 404s, and never type the URL from memory, a typo'd org name produces a false 404): HA never validates icon names, an unknown `mdi:` renders as a **blank icon** rather than an error, and repo tests only check `icon.startswith("mdi:")` (e.g. `test_currency_icon_lookup`), so a fake name passes CI silently. MDI has no icon for Danish/Norwegian/Swedish Crown, Vietnamese dong or Swiss franc — the correct behaviour is to leave such symbols unmapped and let the generic fallback (`CURRENCY_ICON_DEFAULT`, `mdi:cash`, config.py) apply. For the slugify half of (e): HA `slugify()` (python-slugify, separator `_`) lowercases, spaces→`_`, strips punctuation (parentheses removed, not separated) and collapses consecutive separators — verified `"Indevolt SoC kWh"` → `indevolt_soc_kwh` (GH#5683).
- **A component must read read-only mode with `get_arg("set_read_only")`, never `self.base.set_read_only`** (found on PR #5711, Hypervolt, 2026-10-23; verified by reading the code). `PredBat.reset()` sets the attribute to `True`, and only `fetch_config_options()` inside `update_pred()` loads the real value; a component's first `run()` comes before that, so a control loop that releases on read-only releases everything it holds on every restart. `wallbox.py` and `car_charger_control.py` (`control_read_only_now`) still read the attribute first (suspected same restart release, not reproduced); tests miss it because `MockBase` has no `set_read_only`, so only the `get_arg` fallback runs.
- **`re:` arg values are anchored `^...$` — full-match, not prefix match.** `resolve_arg_re()` (`userinterface.py:1324`) builds the matcher as `"^" + pattern + "$"` before `re.search()`, so an anchored pattern cannot over-match a longer entity id that merely shares the prefix: when judging whether a template regex "matches too much" or whether two integration entities collide in a single-entity arg, reason from the `$` side of the pattern, not prefix semantics (GH#5677 review, 2026-10-21; pairs with the first-match-wins rule recorded in the Car charging row).
- **History from `HAHistory` lags up to 2 minutes, and smoothing back-fills the gap, so anything that counts history up to "now" and then moves its marker to now loses the tail** (manual car SoC undercount, found 2026-10-25 on a Hypervolt, 12.0 of 21.2 kWh counted overnight). `HAHistory.run` refreshes its cache every 2 minutes and `minute_data(backwards=True, smoothing=True)` fills the minutes after the newest cached sample with that sample's value, then smooths them back once the next sample arrives. The energy between the newest cached sample and now is therefore not in the history when it is read, and a marker moved to now skips it for good. Keep the marker where it is and count the total since it, less what is already counted (`car_manual_soc_track()`). Verified by replaying a real session through the real function with a 2-minute cache at three phases: the old code counted 9.6 to 12.5 of 17.8 kWh, the fix 17.83 - shipped in PR #5752 (b3928da0, merged 2026-10-25), which keeps the marker where it is and carries the counted-so-far total in the record (`counted`), so each cycle adds only the un-counted delta.
- **Anything reading `self.predict_soc` after a trial simulation gets the nominal path, not pv10/pv90 (PR #5735's review round, verified on the merged code):** `Plan.run_prediction`'s wrapper (plan.py) assigns `self.predict_soc` (plus `car_charging_soc_next`/`iboost_*`) **unconditionally** from the result tuple on both engines — the kernel builds the dict from `soc_out[k * PREDICT_STEP]` — while `Prediction.run_prediction`'s own assignment sits behind `if self.debug_enable or save:` and fires only on debug/saving runs, so a reader landing there wrongly concludes every consumer is broken; and after `run_prediction_metric()` the attribute holds the **nominal** scenario's path (pv90/pv10 run first, nominal last). Keys are minutes-from-now, multiples of 5, spanning 0 .. forecast_minutes−5 in both engines — `predict_soc.get(minute)` returns `None` at exactly `forecast_minutes`, so a "read the SoC at minute X" judgement from a dump stops one step short of the horizon (the plan.py comment says so: "predict_soc stops one step short of the forecast").

## What belongs here, and what does not

The journal is for what a future triage run would otherwise have to rediscover. Test every entry with one question: would it save time on a **different** issue?

- **Keep: techniques and traps.** How to replay a dump (`--redo`), test-order pollution, stale kernel binaries, the macOS local-network block. These apply to every investigation.
- **Keep, tightly: durable facts about a device or API.** One line, no story. "GivEnergy stores rates as a whole percent of capacity, rounded down" is about the hardware, not about the issue that found it.
- **Keep, short and dated: findings that explain a class of report.** "Car not charging because X" is useful when the same symptom comes back. Give the issue or PR number and the date, say how it was verified, and word it as "look here first", so the reader confirms it against the code.
- **Drop: per-issue narrative.** What was tried, what the fix was, why the alternatives were rejected. That belongs in the PR and the issue, where it stays tied to the code it describes. In the journal it goes stale as the code moves.

Entries go stale, so retire them:

- When the code an entry describes has changed, correct or delete the entry. Do not leave it marked as stale.
- Once a fix for a bug entry has shipped, cut the row down to what a reader holding an older log needs ("fixed in PR #N") and drop the story.
- Prefer sharpening an existing entry over adding a second one.

## Adding to this file

When an investigation turns up something that passes the test above, add a row. Keep it short, name the symbol rather than the line number, and cite the issue number so the next reader can check the original.

If you are running under the triage bot you cannot edit this file directly — the clone is `reset --hard` before every flow, so the edit would not survive. Write the finding as a single markdown file in the queue directory named in your system prompt instead; the `/journal-update` flow folds the queue in once a day, re-checking each candidate against current `main` first, and opens a PR for a maintainer to merge.

Whichever route you take, the bar is the same and it is not "interesting": record what you **verified**, say how you verified it, and keep anything you merely suspect explicitly marked as suspected. A wrong entry here is worse than a missing one — every later run trusts this file, so a confident mistake ends up in a comment posted to a real reporter. Correcting or deleting a stale entry counts as much as adding one.
